#2745. Geno Auriemma at a loss after UConn’s loss to rival South Carolina: ‘I have to figure it out’

UNCASVILLE, Conn. — After spending the majority of his career making opposing coaches uncomfortable, legendary UConn coach Geno Auriemma met his match.

Dawn Staley's Gamecocks, the No. 1 team in the country, delivered a tough lesson to No. 7 UConn on Tuesday.

Auriemma was as despondent as he's been all season following UConn's 65-56 loss that dropped the Huskies to 4-3 on the season.

"You might be able to tell that and I'm having a really hard time coaching this team, and I have to figure it out," Auriemma said. "But it'd be nice if I got a little help."

Auriemma seemed embarrassed after losing to No. 5 Michigan by 11 on Nov. 5. He was annoyed when UConn fell to No. 7 Louisville, 69-51, a week and a half later.

Geno Auriemma talks to center Olivia Vukosa (second from left) during UConn's 65-56 loss to South Carolina on Nov. 24, 2026, in Uncasville, Conn. AP

But Auriemma had no explanation for what transpired against the Gamecocks, a team that should bring out the best in his group.

"It's already hard enough to beat the best team in the country when you are actually beating yourself," Auriemma said. "It's incredibly frustrating and just at a loss. Usually I have a pretty good idea but …"

He questioned his team's mental toughness.

The final score may suggest a competitive game. But the second quarter told the real story of the night.

The Huskies, playing without KK Arnold and Kayleigh Heckel, won the first quarter, 16-11. But the Gamecocks took over and never looked back while winning for the seventh time in the past 10 meetings of this rivalry.

South Carolina upped its defensive intensity and started hitting shots. UConn fouled too much, missed layups and allowed Tessa Johnson and Agot Makeer to get hot from deep in that second quarter.

The Gamecocks, despite being without Joyce Edwards (sickness), outscored UConn 27-6 in the second quarter and went on a 15-0 run to close the first half and lead by 16 at halftime.

Johnson scored 32 points, surpassing her career high she set earlier this month in the Gamecocks' win against No. 3 USC.

Geno Auriemma and Dawn Staley shake hands before South Carolina's road victory over UConn. AP

"It feels great to get a win," Staley said. "And it feels super great for a player like Tessa who works extremely hard. What you're seeing is a product of a player that is always in the gym."

Players would like you to believe that South Carolina-UConn is just another game. But Tuesday added another layer in one of college basketball's greatest rivalries.

"It feels good," said Ashlyn Watkins, who stepped up in a big way in Edwards' absence. "It's still early in the season, so we're going to see them again, probably, but we're ready for sure."

Tuesday was the first meeting since Auriemma and Staley's fiery sideline spat in the final seconds of South Carolina's national semifinal win in April.

The two hadn't spoken since the incident, Auriemma told reporters Sunday.

Tessa Johnson, who scored a career-high 32 points, shoots a jumper over Ashlynn Shade during South Carolina's road win over UConn. David Butler II-Imagn Images

The two didn't appear to exchange words pregame or postgame Tuesday.

They did, however, share a brief handshake before and after the game.

This past weekend, Auriemma did his best to downplay any lingering hostility toward Staley.

"I don't coach against other coaches," Auriemma told reporters. "I coach against their players."

But there has to be something about beating a fierce rival that makes a win so much more satisfying, right?

"We play UConn enough to where it is just a game," Staley said. "It's a big game but we've played them enough to where we understand the gravity of the game, we understand the NCAA committee implications and all that goes into it. … But other than that, whether we won or lost, it's not gonna impact us in a way that, like, we're gonna get better, no matter what at this stage of the game. We're not gonna be the team that took the floor today in March or February.

"So it's always good to see how we measure up to a UConn team that's always at the top of the game."

#2743. Has J.K. Dobbins played his final game for the Broncos?

How rare is it for the Denver Broncos to suspend its own player for conduct detrimental to the team? The last such case I could find of this happening was back in August 2009 when Brandon Marshall was suspended by Josh McDaniels. If memory serves me, he punted the football out of practice to show his displeasure with McDaniels and the coaching staff. Marshall did end up playing the whole season that year, but McDaniels traded him soon into the 2010 offseason.

Given that's all the history we have to go on, perhaps J.K. Dobbins' tenure with the Broncos in 2026 isn't done for good and this one game suspension is exactly that: one game.

Mike Klis of 9News noted that something must have happened between Sean Payton's press conference on Monday and the team practice session on Tuesday:

Given how things are playing out in the running back room, I think even after the suspension we won't be seeing Dobbins back out on the field this season. With RJ Harvey poised to return soon and Jonah Coleman showing he has that bell-cow type build to him, I would be comfortable rolling with that group down the stretch.

What do you think: will we see J.K. Dobbins on the field again in 2026 for the Broncos?

#2741. Juve, Milan battle for Bruno! Nuno back, Inter sale: morning news☀️

After Tuesday's Champions League action brought us two good results for Como and Napoli, today it is Inter and Roma's turn.

On-field matters are therefore dominating the conversation in Italy today, although there are plenty of other talking points too.

Watch all Serie BKT matches live on OneFootball for just €49.99. Click here to take advantage of the Black Friday offer and buy the LaB Channel Annual Pass

Let's take a look at the morning's biggest stories.

Juventus and Milan could find themselves battling over one of next summer's biggest transfer names: Bruno Fernandes. As reported by Tuttosport, the two Italian clubs are monitoring the situation of the Manchester United captain, whose contract expires in June 2027. The Portuguese midfielder confirmed that he has received a contract extension offer, but preferred to postpone any discussions about his future to focus on the Red Devils' struggles.

Milan could have an important trump card in Ruben Amorim. The coach knows his compatriot well from their time together at Manchester United, and the pair also share the same agency, a factor that could facilitate any talks. Juventus, meanwhile, are looking for a midfielder with international pedigree. The opportunity to sign a player of Bruno Fernandes' quality without paying a transfer fee appeals to both clubs, but the 32-year-old's high wages are one of the main obstacles to a deal.

Bad news for Milan on the eve of their Europa League clash with Olympiacos. The Rossoneri will be without Luka Modric, who is struggling with a fitness issue that will rule him out of not only the European fixture but also their next league match.

As reported by La Gazzetta dello Sport, the Croatian midfielder has suffered a muscle strain and will also be unavailable against Cagliari. His absence for both games is a major blow for Ruben Amorim, who will have to do without one of his key midfielders: Jashari will take over Modric's playmaking duties.

Tomas Palacios' time at Estudiantes could already be drawing to a close. The Argentine defender, who arrived from Inter in January on a free loan with an option to buy, has established himself as a key player, making 35 appearances, including 32 starts. The Argentine club have until December 31 to exercise their purchase option, set at around €6 million, but River Plate have also entered the race for the defender born in 2003.

According to Germán García Grova of TyC Sports, the Millonarios are in advanced talks with Inter over a permanent deal for the centre-back. Palacios has reportedly already given his approval for negotiations between the clubs to continue, and Saturday's game could be his final appearance in an Estudiantes shirt. A permanent departure could therefore be on the cards for the Inter defender.

Napoli are stepping up their work on contract renewals, with Scott McTominay's future among the club's priorities. As reported by Corriere dello Sport, Giovanni Manna met Colin Murdock, the Scottish midfielder's agent, in Manchester. It was a brief, preliminary meeting, with further talks to follow in the coming weeks. The former Manchester United player's contract expires in June 2028, but the club have been working since last summer to tie him down until at least 2030.

The sticking point remains financial: his representatives are seeking wages of around €10 million. Napoli's management have known about that figure for months and must now find a compromise with the player. Next summer could prove a turning point: without an agreement, Napoli could be forced to consider selling the Scot to avoid losing him on a free transfer in 2028. José Mourinho's Real Madrid remain in the background, having already expressed interest in the midfielder during the last summer transfer window.

Appearing on Belve on Rai2, Francesco Totti reflected on the past. Among the many topics discussed was whether he could have won the Ballon d'Or had he joined Real Madrid.

The former Giallorossi captain also gave his thoughts on the current Roma side and their chances of winning the Scudetto:

"Am I happy to be the last one to have won it? On the one hand, yes, but as a Roma fan, no. This year, maybe..."

Asked about returning to the club, Il Pupone said: "Never say never. The door is never closed; right now we're on two different paths. I'm doing well, and they're doing a great job."

After the two days off granted by Gennaro Gattuso following the win over Lecce, Lazio returned to training at Formello yesterday to prepare for their trip to Torino. The good news for the Biancocelesti coach concerns Mattia Zaccagni, who has now recovered from the strain he suffered against Cagliari on November 1.

There are also encouraging signs regarding Nuno Tavares: the Portuguese player is making swift progress in his recovery, and his chances of being available for the derby on December 13 are increasing. An earlier return against Atalanta on December 6 has not been ruled out either.

Fiorentina's management have not lost faith in Paolo Vanoli, but the upcoming match against Udinese could become a pivotal moment in the Viola's season. Another slip-up could prompt the club to reassess their coaching situation. As highlighted by La Nazione, part of the problem is the difficulty of finding a potential replacement.

One possible internal option would be Aurelio Andreazzoli, who currently coaches Fiorentina's Primavera side. Among out-of-work coaches, however, there are few convincing alternatives: some would require too large a financial investment, while others do not fit the profile Fabio Paratici is looking for. Fiorentina are therefore continuing to back Vanoli, hoping the trip to Udine can prove a turning point.

#2739. Fantasy Basketball Week 5 takeaways: Wemby's historic dominance, key injuries and more from all 30 teams

A quick fantasy basketball breakdown on all 30 teams after Week 5 of the 2026-27 NBA season.

Spurs: Victor Wembanyama dominated Saturday: 50 points, 19 rebounds, 5 blocks and 4 steals in 32 minutes of a 114-91 win over the Pelicans. It got him the High Score of the week, with 97 fantasy points. He's the first player since 1973-74 with a 50/15/5/4 game (since steals and blocks became official stats). It's his second 50 piece this month (52 on Nov. 4), and San Antonio is 15-3 on the season with Wemby leading the way as the top player in fantasy.

Bulls: Josh Giddey posted 23 points, 14 rebounds and a career-high 22 assists in Sunday's 151-145 OT loss to Denver, the league's first 20-20 game this season. Giddey followed up that epic game with a 2-for-11 performance with 4 points, 11 assists, 2 stocks and 3 turnovers on Monday. He was benched in crunch time (again). If you have Giddey, I'd sell. The game-to-game variance is too erratic.

Heat: Bam Adebayo posted 24 points, 15 rebounds and a career-high 12 assists with zero turnovers in Sunday's 123-113 win over OKC with Giannis Antetokounmpo (groin) out. Miami has won seven straight to reach 10-6. Shout-out to Klay Thompson, who averaged 20.7 points and 5.1 3s over a recent seven-game stretch and is closing in on Ray Allen for third all time in 3s. Keep holding Klay in 12s.

Bucks: Tyler Herro set a new career high in Philly on Saturday, dropping 50 points, then added 32 with 8 assists in Monday's win in Toronto to snap a 13-game skid. At 2-16, start prepping for some shakeups in the rotation — which could impact vets like Herro, Kyle Kuzma and Myles Turner. Rookie Brayden Burries (40% rostered) had 14 points, 10 boards, and 4 assists off the bench Monday, and should be rostered in most leagues.

Cavaliers: James Harden dropped 51 in Friday's 128-119 win over Indiana, passing Kobe Bryant for the third-most 50-point games ever (26). Evan Mobley (toe) has missed six straight and is due for re-evaluation this week. Kenny Atkinson has started Tyrese Proctor and Sam Merrill over the past week, but rookie Meleek Thomas (13.3 points in 24 minutes) is still the best streamer here.

Nets: Nolan Traore (right hamstring) will be re-evaluated in 8-10 weeks. Mikel Brown Jr. (19% rostered) got the first crack, starting Monday in Minnesota with his minutes restriction lifted: 17 points, a career-high 7 assists and 5 boards in 29 minutes. Egor Demin shot 3-for-13 off the bench. Brown is the add over Demin. The No. 6 pick has a path to 30 minutes a night.

Lakers: Austin Reaves (left heel) practiced fully on Sunday and is targeting a return on Friday in San Antonio. Quentin Grimes averaged 9.2 points through 12 games, then moved into the starting lineup over the last five games, averaging 24.8 points, 4.6 rebounds, and 3.4 assists. He ranks 31st in 9-cat over that span, with 35.58 fantasy points per game.

Enjoy it now, because he won't see anywhere near 37 minutes a night when Reaves returns. Matisse Thybulle is the keeper. His 39 steals rank second in the league, and he and Wemby are the only players this year with 15+ blocks, 20+ steals and 30+ 3s.

Hawks: Atlanta has won four straight without CJ McCollum, whose left calf strain will be re-evaluated in about four weeks. No. 8 pick Kingston Flemings got next, but don't rush to get him because the Hawks won't play until Friday.

Dyson Daniels, though? He's such a unique fantasy talent. He's posting 9.5 points, 7.4 boards, 5.6 assists and a league-leading 2.9 steals per night. He can't shoot beyond 10 feet, but he's touching nearly every major counting-stats category except blocks and 3s.

Grizzlies: No. 3 pick Cameron Boozer (ankle) will be re-evaluated in four weeks. That hurts. Kris Murray started in his absence on Monday, playing 32 minutes and got 5 stocks. Add him to the watchlist, even though Memphis will likely form a committee between Murray, Taylor Hendricks and Jerami Grant (when he gets back). Make sure Cam Spencer (45%) isn't on waivers.

Clippers: Brandon Ingram started in his debut Monday in Houston and posted 16 points, 7 boards, and 4 assists in 27 minutes. The shooting was unsurprisingly rough (6-for-18, 0-for-6 from three), but 18 shots in his first game since May is a great sign. Marc Stein reported that LA is showcasing vets like Rui Hachimura and Kris Dunn before the trade deadline.

Jazz: Svi Mykhailiuk went OFF Sunday for 39 points and 10 threes, both career highs. I'd rather pick up Ace Bailey, though.

76ers: Philly's five-game win streak ended Monday against Portland with Joel Embiid (illness) out. Embiid's been better, and fantasy managers could take the opportunity to float offers because health is always the biggest question mark with Embiid.

Celtics: Four straight losses. Derrick White is shooting 40.1% from the field and 28.8% from 3, well off his 45.4/38.7 peak. I'm holding in 9-cat, but it's been a disappointing start.

Mavericks: Just two games this week, and two scheduled before the NBA Cup next week. Cut fringe Mavs for players who play more games over the next two weeks.

Knicks: Josh Hart is averaging 6.6 points on 34/27/60 shooting. Woof.

Pistons: Over the past five games, Ausar Thompson ranks 16th in 9-cat. He's making 87.5% of his free throws (albeit low volume, but still!)

Magic: Anthony Black continues to lap Jalen Suggs. Suggs is averaging 9-5-4 with 35/29/73 shooting splits. He's down to 73% rostered, but fantasy managers should stay patient as he ramps up from a bone bruise injury that cost him eight games.

Wizards: The Wizards want Trae Young to focus less on scoring and more on passing. Per Kevin O'Connor, Young had 8 minutes of possession time per game over his first 6 games. It's down to 5 minutes over his last 5 games.

Raptors: Toronto lost to the 2-16 Bucks and sits at 6-11 overall. Kawhi Leonard (knee) is shooting at practice but is still a couple of weeks away from returning. Jakob Poeltl is back on the radar after averaging 11.0 points, 9.3 rebounds, 3.7 assists, 1.3 steals, and 1.3 blocks in 29.7 minutes per game in his last three games.

Pacers: At 5-12 without Tyrese Haliburton, Indiana is reportedly looking to make a move. In the meantime, Kelly Oubre Jr. (36%) should be rostered in all leagues.

Hornets: Brandon Miller and Coby White sat all of OT in a four-point loss to the Lakers last week. Minutes restrictions are costing teams Ws.

Timberwolves: Rudy Gobert ranks 114th in points leagues and 290th in 9-cat. His efficiency near the rim is pacing to his worst since his rookie season — he's 17-for-53 on layups through Monday. Plus, his rebounding and block rates are tumbling. Keep an eye on Joan Beringer.

Rockets: At 13-5, Houston owns the NBA's best offensive rating (121.1) and a plus-8.2 net rating (5th in the league).

Nuggets: Nikola Jokić logged triple-double No. 202 on Sunday. He's seven behind Russell Westbrook's record.

Trail Blazers: Donovan Clingan is finishing 74% at the rim, up from 66% last season.

Thunder: Nearly 80% of Ajay Mitchell's shots are 2s and he's hitting 28.3% from 3. He'll need to extend that range if the Thunder want to right the ship.

Suns: Four straight wins after Monday's OT victory in New Orleans. If Jalen Green was dropped in a points league, pick him up.

Warriors: Stephen Curry (oblique) is due for re-evaluation this week.

Pelicans: New Orleans sits at 5-14, so it's a team to watch by the deadline. Keep holding the young guys, Jeremiah Fears and Derik Queen. Everyone else could be on the block.

Kings: Keegan Murray has 12-plus points and seven-plus boards in four of his last five. Rounding into form? Maybe.

#2738. Utah drops criminal charges against Kalshi founders after company suspended operations in state

Utah on Tuesday dismissed criminal charges against Kalshi and its two co-founders following the decision by the prediction market platform on Monday to suspend operations in the state.

The state originally filed the 14 criminal charges against the company and founders Tarek Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara on Oct. 28, claiming that Kalshi operated an illegal gambling operation in the state.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets which views itself as the sole body that can develop rules over the growing industry — sued Utah the day before the state announced its charges.

Mansour and Lara were slated to appear in a Utah district court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

However, Kalshi suspended operations in Utah on Monday, citing legal uncertainty. Utah Attorney General Derek Brown celebrated the decision in a statement announcing the move to dismiss the state's charges.

"Every company in this space should take note," he said. "If they promote, support, or enable gambling, we will hold them accountable."

Brown's office noted in the statement that dismissal is without prejudice, meaning if Kalshi were to begin operations again in the state the charges could be refiled.

"Good faith jurisdictional disagreements should never be criminalized," Kalshi's general counsel Yaakov Roth said in a post on X. "We stand ready to work with State AGs on reasonable interim measures until courts resolve the dispute over who regulates prediction markets."

While Kalshi faces legal setbacks in Utah, it faced some relief in New York on Monday when a state judge denied an emergency temporary restraining order against the company to halt its operations in the jurisdiction. The court will hear arguments on the matter again in January.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2737. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2736. Nick Young calls Gilbert Arenas and Baron Davis ‘washed’ for never winning an NBA championship

In the continuing conversation about what defines true success in the NBA, Nick Young has now made an emphatic point about players without championships.

With a lot of former players now in the media space, one of the conversations which has come up is how much championship rings matter. There has been an ongoing debate about whether a role player with a ring is more of a winner than an All-Star who was never able to get one.

On Gil's Arena, Nick Young is a perfect example of the former. Despite never having been a star, he did win a championship with the 2017-18 Golden State Warriors. Gilbert Arenas and Baron Davis represent the latter. This dynamic recently led to a heated exchange, with Young going off on the two former All-Stars.

Players' legacies in the modern NBA have become complicated. Individual accolades versus team success is a real conversation, and the basketball world cannot seem to agree on what matters more. Young seems to be clear on what he feels, though, going off on a huge rant about it.

"All the accolades in the world. If you never win, you can't be s***," Young said. "You can never be compared to Jordan and Kobe. You can be top 10, but you'll never be one. If another n**** wins, you get dropped down.

"You can't say you're better than me. You n***** went to other teams, and y'all were f****** trash. Y'all, 3 good years, then became WASHED. Y'all can't beat me. You n***** only had 3 good years and then became WASHED. Stop downplaying my s***. Y'all Vanilla Ice. You can never be Eminem. You'll never be in rooms with champions like me."

This was quite the expressive take from Young, and one that Arenas and Davis naturally disagreed with. Ultimately, championships count heavily for stars who were crucial to getting them. Basketball is a team sport and everyone's contribution counts, but to say that a role player with a ring had a better career than a multiple-time All-Star without one is going a bit too far.

#2735. Congress shreds Nvidia's 'outrageous' decision to bring back Huang: 'Failure of character'

Members of Congress from both parties on Monday expressed outrage and incredulity, and doubled down on their calls for investigations, after Nvidia announced it has reinstated Jensen Huang as its president and CEO.

The resurrection of Huang, who had resigned from the artificial intelligence behemoth just 11 days earlier, raises more questions about the company's decision-making and the Trump administration's national security assessments, on top of existing concerns about Nvidia's advanced AI chips being diverted to China, the lawmakers said.

"It's inexplicable to me why they want this guy back as CEO," Rep. Don Bacon, R-Neb., a China hawk who is retiring from Congress, told CNBC in a phone interview. "To me it's a failure of character."

President Donald Trump told CNBC in an extended interview later Monday morning that he personally intervened to convince Nvidia's board to bring Huang back, saying, "I made the deal."

Bacon, chairman of the House Subcommittee on Cyber, Information Technologies, and Innovation, said he wants the next Congress to conduct thorough oversight, while questioning the Trump administration's motives.

"Somebody's responsible for this, and it may be" Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department is responsible for setting U.S. export controls on AI chips, Bacon said. "But I think we should get to the bottom of this."

Huang, who is scheduled to testify before multiple committees on Capitol Hill starting Dec. 1, is "going to get high scrutiny in a bipartisan way when he comes next week," Bacon said.

Huang's closed-door appearance before a trio of House committees is on the schedule, as the panels seek answers on the national security implications of Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, MS NOW reported. The Senate banking committee announced after Huang's reinstatement that he will appear for a public hearing Dec. 3.

John Fisher of Trident, a spokesman for Huang, declined to comment on the Nvidia CEO's scheduled appearances.

"He better be there," Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., a member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told CNBC about Huang's scheduled Dec. 1 appearance. His reinstatement will only intensify congressional probes into Nvidia and its government connections, Amo predicted.

"I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query, and it makes Howard Lutnick and his dealings — supporting his nepo children's operation at Cantor Fitzgerald — party to inquiry as well as any other administration official that has facilitated a status quo in which chips can be sold to China or Chinese operated intermediaries who don't intend good things for the American people," said Amo, referring to the financial firm run by Lutnick's sons that has business ties to Nvidia.

A Cantor spokesperson didn't comment directly when asked about Amo's comments, saying the firm "holds no material proprietary position in Nvidia" and noting its Nvidia positions "arise primarily from equity swaps executed for clients."

The Commerce Department didn't immediately respond to requests to comment on Bacon's and Amo's remarks.

Nvidia in a statement Monday said it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident that Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

David Rudenstine, a constitutional law professor and national security scholar at Cardozo School of Law, said the frenzy around Huang's comeback is secondary to lingering national security concerns."The real responsibility for national security is with the administration and not with the CEO of a private company," Rudenstine told CNBC."You're opposed to China getting the chips. You recognize that the intermediary companies are sending chips to China, and you don't put a stop to it. I don't get it," he said.

The CEO's reinstatement, while celebrated by the White House and in Silicon Valley, led to heightened calls on Capitol Hill to seek answers on AI chip exports to China and what the Trump administration's role has been in facilitating that.

"We are sending high-end computer chips to China, and yet we're saying we want to win the AI war. It's a contradiction, and it's a failure of leadership in the White House," Bacon told CNBC.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, told CNBC in an emailed statement that Huang's return "proves that under Trump, Vance, and Rubio, national security is for sale to the highest bidder."

"We will all pay the price of letting China plunder America's cutting edge technology. Democrats will be pushing for accountability for anyone in the administration that put corrupt deals over our country's security," Wyden, who is also a Senate Intelligence Committee member, said.

Trump in a Truth Social post Monday added that Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Lawmakers, who have already launched multiple investigations related to Huang, Nvidia and related entities, quickly filed in to question those conclusions.

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., a close Trump ally and a former CEO, criticized the move and called for a congressional briefing "as soon as possible" on Huang's reinstatement.

"Nvidia's board is free to vote however they want, but the federal government owes the American people answers about what actually happened," Scott told CNBC in an emailed statement. "This looks terrible on its face, and it drives home the need for Congress to be informed on the full impact of Jensen Huang's actions on our national security and economy."

Rep. Scott Peters, D-Calif., a member of the House Energy and Commerce Committee, told CNBC that it's still not clear to what degree Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy might have compromised national security.

"The fact that the president of the United States is involved in putting him back in there, presumably because maybe he has stock or he just cares about the stock market, it's really reached a bizarre place," Peters said. "And obviously that man has not learned anything from the election about what people think of him, or he doesn't care."

Monday's move also gave fuel to lawmakers who have already questioned Trump, his administration or Nvidia.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement via the Senate Banking Committee, where she is the top Democrat and is expected to become chair in January.

Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, more tersely questioned the official assurances that Huang poses no risks to national security.

"No national security risk? Based on what?" he wrote on X.

Rep. Raja Krishnamoorthi, D-Ill., who serves on the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, in an X post called Nvidia's decision to reinstate Huang "outrageous."

"We still don't know what information Chinese intelligence may have obtained from him, and neither the government nor Nvidia's own board has completed its investigation," wrote Krishnamoorthi, who is also a member of the House Oversight and Intelligence panels.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan, Garrett Downs and Luke Fountain contributed to this report.

#2734. Sandro Mamukelashvili was in the gym with Flava Flav trying to break out of his slump

Since going a combined 10-of-16 from deep against the Atlanta Hawks and Milwaukee Bucks, which raised his season-long 3-point percentage to 41.2, Sandro Mamukelashvili has not made a triple try. Over the Los Angeles Lakers' last five games, the Georgian big man is 0-of-16 beyond the arc.

Prior to Monday's win over the Utah Jazz, head coach JJ Redick detailed the approach he's taking to try and help Mamukelashvili snap out of his drought from deep, which includes his own experiences as an NBA player and wisdom shared by one of the league's sharpest coaches.

"I think, right now, with Sandro is us getting to just know each other and understand each other. Part of my job is to understand the psyche of each and every one of my players and why is that important? So that in moments like this I can help them," Redick told reporters. "[Erik Spoelstra] told me one time it's more art than science, Really, just trying to help him. I shared with him some stories and we actually went on Basketball-Reference the other night and went through some game logs for my career. I shot 44%' and he's like, 'Oh, I know..I went and looked. I went and looked.' So, I think him and I are on the same page."

Following Wednesday's loss to the Jazz, when Mamukelashvili missed all four of his long-range attempts and was 1-of-10 overall from the floor, he got shots up at the practice facility until 2:30 a.m. Joining Mamukelashvili in the dark of night was Flava Flav, who subdued his typically boisterous personality as his friend aimed to snap out of a shooting funk.

"He's always so loud, but he saw how intense I was," Mamukelashvili told reporters on Monday morning. "So he was like **whispers** 'yeahh boyy'"

Mamukelashivili will hope his early morning shooting session alongside Flava Flav and conversations with Redick finally turn the tide when Los Angeles returns to the floor Friday night against the San Antonio Spurs.

#2733. Peterson: Here’s what Bessent’s about-face on Jensen Huang reveals about Trump and China

For a minute there, it seemed like someone in the Trump administration might actually be willing to stand up to China.

For about a week and a half, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was the face of an emerging harder line on Nvidia over the potential risks its co-founder Jensen Huang's affairs posed to national security. Bessent was giving voice to China hawks who had felt sidelined by the Trump administration's willingness to sell artificial intelligence chips to  China.

They see China's AI program as a major threat, and want to restrain its growth by cutting off the supply of advanced chips to China. The resignation of Huang, who pressed the Trump Administration to allow the exports, seemed to signal that the hawks had found new influence in Washington.

By this weekend, Bessent had softened his public resistance to Huang's reinstatement. That came just in time for Nvidia's board to reappoint Huang as CEO at President Donald Trump's urging.

Whether any real damage was done to national security isn't clear. But it is safe to say that the incoming Democratic majority in Congress won't take Trump at his word when he said on CNBC Monday that the Department of Justice "did a pretty good investigation" that exonerated Huang.

Congress will look deeply into the worries Bessent himself raised about Huang's acknowledged romantic relationship with a woman The New York Times and others have reported was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia's board said it had received assurances from top U.S. officials and had concluded his return wasn't a national security risk.

A top concern is whether China was able to use the affair to help divert Nvidia's high-end artificial intelligence chips toward helping its military. Bessent in a Nov. 13 interview with CNBC outlined the stakes.

"Any leakage is very frustrating because it hurts the — it hurts our great companies and in the AI race it gives the Chinese an advantage," he said.

Bessent said that his department and others had wanted to blacklist Megaspeed, the company previously run by Alice Huang, Jensen Huang's paramour. But "Commerce has the pen," Bessent said.

The Commerce Department is headed by Secretary Howard Lutnick, Bessent's rival.

Putting Huang back at Nvidia wasn't originally Bessent's preference. He said in a Fox interview on Nov. 15 that the board should "go outside the company" to find a new leader. But by this past weekend, Bessent had apparently gotten on board with Trump's wishes.

"Jensen is on the outside now," Bessent told CBS on Sunday, in trying to explain his apparent policy shift.

Commerce has since taken steps to tighten some restrictions meant to prevent U.S. chips from helping China advance in the AI race.

But there is bipartisan worry about the Nvidia episode. "I think we need to catalog the damage that's been done," Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., told The Wall Street Journal. He said Lutnick had not responded to letters he sent saying the administration should be "taking the threats of communist China more seriously."

But Trump has clearly put an upper limit on how aggressively anyone in his administration can push back on China. The president said Monday that he was swayed by the pageantry in his visit to China last week.

Xi "met me at the plane, which is something they've never done, which was a sign," Trump said.

Trump said he felt like he needed to intervene to back Huang because the stock market was plummeting in the wake of Nvidia's troubles — a development he also blamed on Democrats' midterm victory.

"I watched our country lose $5 trillion because of a bad election," he said. "If I didn't get involved, you'd probably have a very sad Nvidia for a long time to come." Now, its stock performance will be "unbelievable."

Bessent on Sunday played down the market risks. The company "continues to be fantastically profitable, and there is near unlimited demand for their chips worldwide," he told CBS.

Lutnick has embraced Trump's transactional urges. He has backed deals in which the U.S. takes equity stakes in companies such as Intel, saying the taxpayer deserves some of the upside.

Lutnick in January said he had heard Huang pitch Trump on allowing Nvidia to sell its relatively more advanced H200 chips into China.

"There are strong — two sides. We shouldn't sell them any or we should sell them." Where does he stand? "Right in the middle," Lutnick told Bloomberg. Trump decided to go ahead with the deal.

Bessent has said he is "neither a hawk nor a dove" on China, and is simply trying to manage the ultra-sensitive relationship between the world's two biggest economies. But the upshot of the Huang affair seems to be that Bessent wasn't able to sway the president on a matter that seemed cut-and-dried just days ago.

Trump said he discussed whether Huang should return to Nvidia with Lutnick, Bessent and Vice President JD Vance. "Howard Lutnick was very good. Very, he's very professional," Trump said on CNBC Monday.

"I call them the opposites, Scott and Howard," Trump added, before declaring them "both good."

#2732. Notre Dame’s place in the CFP rankings is still very fluid

Notre Dame fans learned all too well last year that the college football playoff rankings are something more of an art form than science. It's funny how a body that proclaims it uses a handful of metrics eventually falls apart due to odd formatting and embedded notions about who is actually good — and made up reasons to support made up reasons.

The art is terrible, and the math never has the work actually shown.

Notre Dame is currently No. 4 with one game left for them, but two weeks of college football still left to be played. More than any other team in the rankings, Notre Dame is vulnerable because they are who they are. The Irish have a seat, a strong voice, ands a vote at the CFP table — but they are certainly outnumbered in sentiment.

But that's the preamble for disaster which is damn near impossible right now.

Still… Notre Dame could still find themselves ranked lower than No. 4 when the final rankings are released, but they could also find themselves as the No. 2 team. It's weird man, but there are still all of these games to be played, and we don't know how this will all end up. It frustrates me to no end that so many look at these brackets they publish based off of the latest set of rankings and call them a "projection." It's not a projection — it's a reflection of the current seeding of which NO ONE expects to be the same in a few weeks.

There's also the argument/debate between Notre Dame fans about what's better, a bye or a first round game?

There were several questions in the comments of the announcement post, that I want to answer.

I think if it's Wake Forest that wins the ACC championship with a 12-1 record, they will jump into the top 4.

Texas will drop one spot if they lose the SEC Championship.

Of course… Duke could beat Wake Forest and Texas A&M could beat Texas this week, and none of that matters.

The rankings are still very, very fluid and all Notre Dame can do is win on Saturday and see how this lines up. A win guarantees a place in the CFP, and that is all they were expecting last year before they were screwed out of the opportunity.

#2730. Anthropic shares fall below IPO price. Traders see even more pain

Anthropic shares are back trading below the IPO price after a 13% sell-off Tuesday and a 1.5% drop Monday, despite a broad rally in most big tech artificial intelligence names. Trading under $76 on Tuesday, the stock's now 8.5% below its initial public offering level and 34% beneath its intraday high of $115.

It took just four days for ANT to fall under its offering price, compared with 21 trading days for SpaceX, which rallied out of the gate.

Options volume on the stock is picking up as the shares drop and implied volatility jumps to 96, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Almost 500,000 options traded by noon ET on Tuesday for a total premium of about $250 million, the sixth-most active single-stock options in the market, SpotGamma data shows.

Almost 300,000 puts traded versus about 200,000 calls, but the sentiment implied by the trades was not overtly bearish, and judging by the biggest single trades of the day, traders are divided on what happens next.

The biggest single option trade of the day was a $7.5 million purchase of almost 11,000 75-strike puts expiring in mid-December. The trader paid above the ask, suggesting an eagerness to get the trade done at a cost of $6.85, according to SpotGamma. With Anthropic trading at $77, the position needs Anthropic to drop 11% by expiry to pay off.

"Anthropic remains bumpy as investors add risk to other names," said noted tech analyst Dan Ives.

Only one obvious call purchase appeared among the top 10 trades: a 3,000-lot purchase of 85-strike calls expiring Jan. 15 that cost $7.10 each, for a total of $2.2 million. That trade needs Anthropic to rally 19% within the next two months to break even.

Arguably the most interesting action was a string of almost a dozen put sales, ranging from $400,000 to $1.6 million each, all within moments of each other, selling the 120-strike puts expiring Dec. 18. The puts bring in $40 each, meaning the breakeven for the trade is at $80. Selling in-the-money puts in high-volatility stocks is often a way for traders to get long stock without the capital requirements of buying the underlying equity.

#2727. Quick strikes in first half sink Houston Dynamo in playoff-opening loss to San Jose Earthquakes

Poor set piece defending doomed the Houston Dynamo in Tuesday's playoff match versus the San Jose Earthquakes.

The Earthquakes scored two first-half goals off a corner and a free kick Tuesday to dispatch the Dynamo 2-1 in Game 1 of the Western Conference first-round series at PayPal Park in San Jose, Calif.

"In the playoffs, everybody has to be at their best and then you have to play a smart, intelligent game," Houston coach Ben Olsen said. "Those two things have to add up if you're going to win in the playoffs."

Game 2 will be at 1:30 p.m. Saturday at Shell Energy Stadium, with the Dynamo needing a victory to force a deciding Game 3 in San Jose.

San Jose captain Ronaldo Vieira opened the scoring with a short-range header in the 11th minute off a corner kick. Winger Timo Werner whipped in a cross to the front of the goal. Viera, in position to screen goalkeeper Jonathan Bond and left unmarked by any defenders, headed it into the net without impediment.

"That should never happen," Olsen said. "I don't think we've given up a near stick corner all year."

Eduard Löwen doubled the advantage with a beautiful long-range free kick 13 minutes later. He had already forced a great save out of Bond earlier in the match on a free kick but this shot, from around 32 yards out, curved and dipped to his left and out of the reach of Dynamo goalie.

Löwen's goal comes just a day after winning the MLS Comeback Player of the Year award. Lowen's wife, Ilona, died earlier in the year of Stage 4 brain cancer. Tuesday's game was the 29-year-old German's first playoff game since Ilona had been diagnosed with cancer in 2024.

"Credit to the goalkeeper on the first one, that was an amazing save," Löwen said on Apple TV after the match. "I thought I'd go the same way again, but then I thought maybe I try the other way. Thank God it worked out."

Houston's best chance in the first half came when the ball found Héctor Herrera outside the box with San Jose goalkeeper Earl Edwards Jr. off his line. But Löwen cleared Herrera's looping shot from in front of the goal.

Herrera started the match just days after being charged with driving while intoxicated last weekend in Montgomery County.

The Dynamo came out stronger in the second half but a goal remained elusive until Duncan McGuire scored in the 80th minute. Mateusz Bogusz crossed in a ball from the left wing that McGuire redirected it into the net.

It was only the second goal for McGuire since joining Houston from Orlando City SC in the summer.

"Great ball in from (Bogusz)," McGuire said. "The ball was perfect, and it came off well. I'm glad to get a goal."

But the Dynamo had no time to build any momentum off the goal before starting center back Agustin Resch got red carded for taking down Ousseni Bouda outside the Houston box after turning the ball over just a minute later.

Resch, a rookie who was sixth on the team in minutes this season, will be suspended for Saturday's Game 2 at Shell Energy Stadium.

The Dynamo need to win to force a winner-take-all Game 3 on Dec. 2 back in Northern California.

"It was a tricky week," Olsen said. "I don't want to get into all that, but it's not an excuse. But hopefully, we can all look ourselves in the mirror, get back and understand that all we have to do is win a game to get back here and try this again."

#2726. MMBets Game of the Week: Mavs and Nuggets battle for Group A supremacy

The Dallas Mavericks are set to battle with the Denver Nuggets in an NBA Cup Group play game on Tuesday night. These two teams find themselves at the top of the Western Conference's Group A, and the winner of this contest will very likely secure their spot in the knockout stage of the NBA Cup. Should be an excellent night of hoops, and we've got you covered with our MMBets Game of the Week betting preview.

#2725. How Meta is using Instagram to get an ad boost from its Muse personal AI agent

Meta's ad-free Muse personal artificial intelligence agent is already helping the social media giant boost its advertising business, and it's happening through Instagram, multiple people told CNBC.

Instagram is a major advertising revenue engine for the social media giant, and the company is leveraging existing habits on the platform by prompting users to organize their saved posts in Muse.

About 20% of daily Instagram users save at least one post to the app per day, according to a person familiar with the details, who asked not to be named because the information is not public. Many of those saved items, the person said, are either shoppable items or ads.

A senior executive told CNBC that Muse, even without ads, "is still very valuable for our ads business because it raises conversions for advertisers." It does this by reducing friction in the shopping process and allowing users to go back to items later, said the executive, who asked not to be named because the details have not been reported publicly.

"It gives advertisers a way to get more ROI for the dollars they're already spending, and that's all because Muse is helping people shop," the executive said.

Mark Zuckerberg announced in Meta's last earnings call that 25% of Muse's users shop via the agentic tool — it's the most common use case for Muse — and the average purchase price is over $200. Zuckerberg said the company plans to take a small fee from transactions made through the agentic platform.

All that information about what people are shopping for with their agent is a valuable asset to target ads more precisely within Instagram. If consumers complete purchases with their Muse, that builds Meta's new revenue stream.

Meta's efforts to drive transactions seem to be paying off, and the buzz around Muse has made it a leader in agentic shopping.

In the week ending Nov. 22, 51% of all agentic orders were done by Meta's Muse, according to transactions measured by Forter Intelligence, an e-commerce analytics and cybertechnology company.

Meta dominates agentic AI shopping market share, according to Forter, which said that SpaceX's Grok held a 24% market share in that same time period, followed by 22% to OpenAI's ChatGPT. The remaining 3% is divided between Google and startup Instinct.

Meta is expected to eventually launch ads in Muse, though multiple people said that it's not under any financial pressure to do so and tends to take its time building an audience before monetizing it.

The people said that putting ads in the Muse feed, which is more akin to a newsfeed instead of responses to specific queries, would be a natural way for Meta to test ads on the platform.

#2723. St. John's outlasts Ducks in Players Era quarterfinal

The Oregon Ducks men's basketball team looked pretty good in the first three games. But then reality set in.

After a loss to Oregon State, the Ducks went on to Las Vegas, where St. John's exposed every single weakness this team has in a 90-79 defeat in the Players' Era Championship.

As a result, Oregon gets "rewarded" by getting to play No. 5 Texas Tech on Thanksgiving.

The Ducks were down by more than 15 points for most of the second half, but Oregon was able to stage a rally to cut the deficit into single digits towards the end of the game. Unfortunately for the Ducks, St. John's figured out they were able to get the rim almost at will to cut down any rally Oregon try to stage

For the entire first half and much of the second half, St. John's press baffled Oregon all night, as the Ducks committed 15 turnovers. The Red Storm also had superior speed on both ends of the floor, which led to contested shots for Oregon and easy buckets for the Red Storm. The Ducks' lack of defensive fundamentals and footwork led to a bunch of layups for St. John's. At times, it seemed as if defense was optional for Oregon.

There was one bright spot for the Ducks with the revelation of Jerry Easter II. The transfer from USC played sparingly in the first four games, but in this contest, Easter scored 14 points to lead Oregon, 13 of those coming in the second half alone. Jasper Johnson added 13, and Fred Payne scored 12 for the Ducks.

But that was not nearly enough to counter the Red Storm's offensive attack. Ian Jackson led all scorers with 21 points. St. John's ended up with four players in double figures. Once the Red Storm figured out they could get to the rim off that will, it was fairly easy for them to score. The end of the game with 52 points in the paint.

Oregon did shoot 51 percent from the field, but finished just 20-of-29 from the free-throw line and a paltry 3-of-15 from the three-point line.

There's been a lot of talk of how the Ducks have a lot of depth, which they do, but Head Coach Tony Stubblefield is going to have to cut down that rotation to eight or nine players in order to develop some chemistry. That showed up in a big way in this game, as Oregon just had nine assists on the 28 baskets made.

#2720. Earthquakes make successful return to MLS playoffs with victory over Houston

Before Tuesday, it had been 14 long years since the San Jose Earthquakes had given their fans the experience of thunderous, high-pressure atmosphere of playoff soccer at home - and 23 even longer years since Quakes fans bore witness to a home playoff victory, when Landon Donovan rocketed a Golden Goal past Kansas City Wizards goalie Pat Onstead in sudden-death overtime to send San Jose to the MLS Cup they would eventually capture.

So it was only right that in their first playoff match since 2020 and their first ever in PayPal Park, the Earthquakes gave the dedicated fans who ignored oncoming storms and persevered through mind-numbing I-280 traffic a 2-1 victory over the Houston Dynamos to take the first leg of the best-of-three Round 1 series.

And the Quakes didn't keep their fans in suspense, getting the long-awaited party started early.

After left midfielder Beau Leroux won a corner kick patrolling the Dynamos box, ace striker Timo Werner delivered the ball to the near post, where central midfielder and captain Ronaldo Vierra headed it in, prompting the San Jose Ultras section to whip off their shirts and as San Jose took a 1-0 lead 11 minutes into the match.

Just 13 minutes later, as the drummers in the Epicentro fan section banged louder and louder, MLS Comeback Player of the Year Eduard Löwen squared up for a free kick a few yards outside the Dynamos box and rifled the ball over the Houston defenders, ricocheting the rock off the left post to extend the San Jose lead to 2-0.

And though the stadium grew tense after the Dynamos' Duncan McGuire found the back of the net at the 80-minute mark, San Jose held on through regulation and five minutes of stoppage time to collect its first home playoff victory in PayPal Park's history, earning a standing ovation from the Quakes fans.

Game 2 of the series will be Saturday in Houston. Should Game 3 be necessary, it would be played at PayPal Park on Dec. 2.

#2719. Anthropic closes below $83 IPO price, but analysts are still largely positive

Shares of Anthropic closed down 6.5% on Tuesday, sending the stock below the artificial intelligence company's $83 IPO price.

The AI lab's stock has been sinking steadily since opening at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, after Anthropic raised $81.3 billion in the largest offering of all time. Investors eagerly awaited Anthropic's debut in hopes that the company would revitalize the broader AI trade, which has been volatile in recent weeks over concerns about the feasibility of a multitrillion-dollar infrastructure buildout.

Chip stocks Nvidia, Advanced Micro Devices and Intel, as well as neoclouds CoreWeave, IREN and Nebius, all fell during intraday trading on Tuesday. The market is still reeling after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, a dramatic reversal from his ouster earlier this month. Nvidia shares surged more than 30% on Monday, but pared those gains Tuesday.

Analysts at Canaccord Genuity said Tuesday that they're focused on Anthropic's long-term potential. They said they believe that the company is building "one of the most important enterprise technology franchises of the AI era" and that the "opportunity is considerably larger than traditional software spending."

Canaccord Genuity initiated coverage of Anthropic with a buy rating and a $135 price target.

"The risks are unusually large, particularly around infrastructure commitments and the durability of competitive differentiation, but we believe the combination of exceptional growth, emerging earnings capacity, and an attractive entry valuation support a compelling investment opportunity," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

Wedbush analysts initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating and a $120 price target on Monday. They said Anthropic went public in a "tumultuous market" as debate around the AI buildout continues, but that they expect the company to navigate the competitive environment and "capture a uniquely large addressable market."

"While there's going to continue to be many ups and downs, and winners and losers as this ecosystem gets built, in our view there is no real question that it does get built over time," the analysts wrote in a Monday note. "The genie is out of the bottle. And ANT is a strong play on that theme."

Analysts at Oppenheimer initiated coverage of Anthropic with an outperform rating on Tuesday, and they set a $150 price target for the stock.

They said their "bullish stance" on the company stems from their belief that Anthropic will capture growing enterprise AI spending, that its improving compute efficiency lowers cost per task and that its multi-chip strategy optimizes price performance. The analysts added that Anthropic's focus on the enterprise also relies less on consumer subsidies, and that rising customer adoption creates "a substantial runway" for the company.

"While competition and compute commitments remain risks, we believe Anthropic can translate its leadership into durable growth and operating leverage," the analysts wrote in a Tuesday note.

#2718. What are the worst losses for each Super Bowl-winning team?

The Minnesota Vikings are coming off a particularly tough showing in a 35-10 loss to the San Francisco 49ers in Mexico City on Sunday night. One thing I hear a lot after games like this is, "Well, you're definitely not a Super Bowl contender if you can lose a game by 25 points."

So that got me thinking — what's the worst loss each Super Bowl team had in the year they won the big game?

The last team to lose a game by 25-plus points and win a Super Bowl in the same season? It wasn't that long ago — the Tom Brady-led Tampa Bay Buccaneers six seasons ago lost 38-3 in Week 9 to the New Orleans Saints.

The average deficit for the 59 previous Super Bowl winners who lost a game — pop that champagne, remaining surviving 1972 Miami Dolphins — was 15.4 points.

I don't know that any of these numbers truly mean anything, but it felt too interesting not to share.

#2716. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2715. Consumer confidence shoots up after Democrats' midterm victories — though inflation worries persist

Consumers grew significantly more optimistic in November in the wake of the midterm elections, with a growing number of Americans now seeing improvement in the labor market and expecting inflation to cool.

The Conference Board survey released Tuesday showed improvement across the board, with the headline confidence index jumping to 103.3, up 11.4 points from October and much better than the Dow Jones consensus forecast for 92.2.

Confidence among Democrats and independent voters improved sharply, the Conference Board said, while confidence among Republican respondents fell slightly. Still, confidence among Republicans remained the highest among the three groups.

While respondents "continued to express frustration with high prices and rising costs," they also "cited hope for the general economy," said Fergus McCormick, the board's senior economist for global indicators.

The survey comes with the Federal Reserve continuing to try to bring down inflation, which has run above the central bank's 2% target for some 5½ years. The Fed is expected to raise its benchmark interest rate by a quarter percentage point when it meets again in two weeks.

Despite the lingering worries about the cost of living — a centerpiece issue during the midterms — the survey's present situation and expectations indexes both posted strong gains. Inflation expectations for 12 months from now fell for the second straight month but remained high at 5.7%.

The survey's closely watched gap between jobs considered "plentiful" versus "hard to get" widened to 12.4 percentage points. The "plentiful" side rose to 30.8% from 26.2% while "hard to get" fell to 18.4%, down 3 percentage points.

The survey results are the second in the past week to reveal that Americans are growing more optimistic about the economy. The University of Michigan's November survey, released Friday, showed a 7.3% increase since October, with a similar partisan split.

#2714. ‘Can’t Wait For This Coaching Staff To Be Out’ — Irritated Colts Fans Question Harsh Film Room Treatment of Anthony Richardson

The Indianapolis Colts are turning to Anthony Richardson at quarterback after waiving Daniel Jones, giving the 2023 first-round pick another opportunity to establish himself as the team's long-term option.

However, comments from offensive coordinator Jim Bob Cooter about Richardson's preparation have since prompted a wave of frustration among Colts fans, with several questioning how the coaching staff has handled the quarterback.

Richardson is preparing for his first start since the 2024 season after spending much of the current campaign behind Jones. Despite Jones struggling at times, head coach Shane Steichen stuck with him until the organization decided to make a change.

Cooter recently discussed Richardson's experience in the quarterback room, pointing out that he has had the chance to observe several players and their weekly preparation routines. The offensive coordinator suggested that seeing different approaches could give Richardson a broader understanding of how quarterbacks prepare for opposing defenses.

"We've obviously had an interesting four years at (QB). But Anthony's got to watch a lot of different guys go through their weekly process … of preparing for an opponent," Cooter said.

"It can be nice to have that 10,000-foot view," Cooter added.

The comments quickly drew criticism from Colts fans, with some questioning why the coaching staff continues to insinuate that Richardson's prep is lagging behind as he gets ready to return to the starting lineup.

One frustrated fan wrote, "Can't wait for this coaching staff to be out."

Another questioned whether the repeated references to preparation suggested Richardson had struggled with that aspect of his game. "Dude, AR must be awful at preparation for how much they harp on it," the user wrote.

"I think we're in a really good place. I know there is a lot of stuff out there, but me and him, we still talk regularly, and I'm excited for his opportunity. He's matured over these years. He puts in good work, and now he gets another opportunity. I'm really looking forward to seeing him go out and compete on Sunday," Steichen said.

Richardson's return also gives Steichen an opportunity to test out the new dynamics in offense through the quarterback's mobility. The Colts have struggled this season, with their passing game ranking near the bottom of the league.

According to PFN's Offense Impact Metric, Indianapolis ranks 29th with a 66.4 grade. Its passing offense has a 58.4 grade, ranking 32nd, while the running game has been considerably better with an 82.6 grade, good for 10th.

Richardson will get his first opportunity to lead the offense against the New York Giants on Sunday.

#2713. James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers

James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James likes to refer to the NBA season as a "marathon."

A few miles in, the Sixers lead the Eastern Conference pack.

With 17 regular-season contests down of 82, the team sits at 14-3, which is 2.5 games ahead of the second-seeded Celtics. The Sixers squeaked out their latest win, a 132-129 decision Saturday over the cellar-dwelling Bucks, but the bottom line is they've become accustomed to finding many routes to victory.

"We want to build habits," James said. "We want to try to take steps forward every game that we get the opportunity. I know sometimes it doesn't happen that way, but we want to do it.

"We want to strive to be as great as we can offensively, defensively and on special teams. A win is a win, but there's ways to win and ways to try to continue to build habits over the course of a marathon."

All sorts of Sixers games have gone down to the wire and they've won every single one.

The team is an incredible 12-0 in clutch situations, defined as games with a point differential of five or under in the final five minutes. The Sixers rank third in the NBA in clutch offensive rating (140.3) and third in clutch net rating (plus-42.0).

"It's good to build that part of our game," James said. "Obviously, we wanted to close the game out a lot better. But a team like that, we know they're going to be scrappy. They're going to play as hard as they can for 48 minutes. … We can learn from that."

James was the centerpiece of the Sixers' fourth-quarter offense Saturday. He scored 13 of his 21 points between the 7:46 and 3:13 mark of the fourth.

The four-time MVP got two buckets on no-nonsense drives and another on an after-timeout isolation hoop over rookie Brayden Burries. James also scored three times on a designed Horns action where he brought the ball up, used Tyrese Maxey's back screen at the elbow and caused serious problems for Milwaukee in the paint.

"We continue to add things to the half-court offense and also to the late-game package," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "That's something we added a few game ago. It's one of those things where, especially if we're in the bonus, we've got some big bodies that can cut down the lane and have feel for timing. It was working. Just keep going to it."

James has grown in comfort with Nurse's coaching style. He knows the Sixers expect him to be a de facto coach on the floor, too.

"I'm just trying to be there for the team in any capacity they want," he said. "Just trying to be an extension of his calls and his commands out there on the floor. Trying to make his life and his job a little bit easier as well.

"But it's been great, not only with him but the whole coaching staff. They've welcomed me with open arms and allowed me to kind of be the voice for the team every day, and take that responsibility."

After a tremendous first impression defensively and some subpar offensive nights, the Sixers' numbers have recently evened out.

The team is 12th in offensive rating outside of garbage time and 12th in defensive rating, per Cleaning the Glass. In essence, the Sixers have been a bit above average on both ends of the floor and downright brilliant in the closing minutes.

It's all added up to an early lead in a very long race.

"I think we've bought into the pass," James said. "We have so many guys that can score the ball at elite levels. … In order for us to be a super-complete team, I think the power of the pass is very infectious. And it's also key for our other guys, to make sure they feel in a rhythm and they feel confident. We've done that from Day 1 and I think it's gotten better and better. … And I think our defense has been really good for the majority of the season.

"(Today) it wasn't so great, but we've been very good defensively throughout the course of the season. … I don't want to say there's anything bad. There's so many things for us to do better for sure. There's games where we can defensive rebound a lot better and there's games where we can not turn the ball over (as much). And there's games where we can be a little more phsyical defensively at the point of attack like (today).

"I wouldn't say anything has been bad, though. That's a good thing. We want to continue to have room for improvement and we don't want to be great in November. That would be weird."
