#2712. Gold is doing something strange. Here's one way to play it

Gold has stopped trading like a haven and started trading more like a market index this year. The past week has underscored it. Fears around AI, a hawkish Fed, and a stronger dollar sent many investors rushing to raise cash, and gold was sold along with stocks. GLD (SPDR Gold Shares) and the S&P 500 both slid around 9%. Gold then shared in the market's Monday rebound, with GLD up about 3% as the S&P 500 jumped nearly 5%.

Over long timeframes, gold's returns tend to move independently of the S&P 500's. From 2004 to 2025, the correlation between their daily returns has been just 0.05 (+1 means the two move in lockstep and -1 means they move in opposite directions). That relationship fluctuates, and 2026 has seen an unusually strong link. The correlation has been about 0.41 so far in 2026. Its rolling three-month reading peaked near 0.80 in June and now sits at 0.54, still higher than about 95% of rolling three-month readings since 2005. Since the end of August, GLD has fallen on 77% of the S&P 500's down days, compared with roughly half historically.

The options market is also behaving unusually. Gold options typically price calls richer than puts (call skew). Like other commodities, gold is prone to sharp rallies, and investors use calls to bet on them or hedge against macroeconomic risks. However, gold options have mostly shown the reverse this year, with puts currently trading richer than calls (put skew). It's a sign investors are paying a premium for protection against further declines, which is unusual for gold and more typical of stock options.

Those richer puts come as uncertainty around gold runs high heading into December. The PCE inflation report arrives Wednesday, followed by the jobs report next week and the Fed's decision and CPI the week after. GLD's implied volatility, a measure of how much the options market expects the fund to move, is now higher than on about 80% of days over the past year. Options are pricing in an expected move of about 6%, or roughly $20, by Dec. 18. With gold options pricier than usual and puts unusually rich, that presents an interesting setup for traders willing to sell premium.

Based on Monday's close, I'm selling the Dec. 18 310/300 put spread and 360/370 call spread in GLD for a credit of roughly $2.24.

This is a directionally neutral trade. Gold has been swinging with the market and is down about 33% from its January high. With the Fed and inflation data ahead, it's hard to say whether gold has bottomed or has further to fall into mid-December. A neutral position can collect the elevated premium without picking a direction, and the richer put side adds more.

The short strikes sit just outside the range the options market is pricing for Dec. 18, roughly 7% to 8% from Monday's close. That leaves room for a continued selloff or an extended rebound. The trade reaches its maximum profit of $224 if GLD closes between $310 and $360 on Dec. 18. It carries a 62% theoretical probability of profit and can show a gain before expiration if GLD remains relatively rangebound.

The maximum loss is capped by the long options and reaches $776 if GLD closes below $300 or above $370, 10% to 11% from Monday's close. Defining risk is especially important with the upcoming catalysts and recent price volatility in gold. GLD has had 30 daily moves of more than 3% this year, including a 10.3% drop on Jan. 30. The trade breaks even at $307.76 on the downside and $362.24 on the upside.

A close between $360 and $370 can assign the short call and leave a short position in the fund, so investors looking to avoid that should consider managing the trade before expiration. If GLD finishes between $300 and $310, the short put can be assigned, creating an obligation to buy 100 shares per spread at $310. After the credit, that's a cost basis of about $307.76, roughly 8% below Monday's close.

#2711. How the Sixers can advance to the NBA Cup knockout round

The Joel Embiid-less Sixers got pasted Monday by the Portland Trail Blazers, but they have a golden opportunity to wash that bad taste out of their mouths. Their next two games are their final two games in the NBA Cup's group-play stage, and they still aren't eliminated from earning a spot in the knockout round.

Here's a glimpse at the current standings heading into Wednesday's games:

And here's a reminder of how the tiebreakers work, which very well could come into play to determine the winner of East Group B:

Point differential in group play (not including overtime)

Total points scored in group play (not including overtime)

Random drawing if none of those tiebreakers resolve the tie

It might look like the Heat are sitting pretty at 2-0 with a plus-32 point differential, but they face the Sixers on Wednesday and the New York Knicks on Friday. There's a non-zero chance they're 2-2 by the end of that stretch, which would likely end their chances of winning Group B.

So, what do the Sixers need to emerge as the winners of Group B? First, they have to sweep the Heat and Pacers this week. Anything short of a 3-1 record will all but guarantee their elimination.

Even if they do win both games, the Cleveland Cavaliers are lurking as a roadblock since the Cavs have the head-to-head tiebreaker over them. The Sixers would need the Knicks to beat the Cavs on Wednesday, which would push them down to 2-2.

If the Knicks do beat the Cavs and the Sixers beat both the Heat and Pacers, they will win Group B and advance to the knockout round. If the Sixers beat both the Heat and Pacers but the Cavs beat the Knicks, the Cavs will win Group B, and the Sixers will have to hope to sneak into the knockout round with a wild-card spot.

The Sixers' minus-19 point differential won't help them in the wild-card race, though. They'll need to run up the score against both the Heat and Pacers to give themselves a realistic chance of securing a wild-card bid.

If the Sixers fall short of the knockout round, it once and for all proves that they're frauds this year, obviously. They'll also have two games added to their schedule between Dec. 6-10 based on a "formulaic approach" that uses the group-play standings in each conference, according to the NBA.

"Two of the 22 games will be scheduled cross-conference since there will be an odd number of teams in each conference that do not advance to the quarterfinals. These cross-conference games will be scheduled between bottom- finishing teams in the Group Play stage subject to travel constraints, and no team will play more than one of its two games cross-conference. The other 20 games will be scheduled within conference featuring teams that are otherwise scheduled to play each other three times over the course of the season wherever possible."

The Sixers have never advanced to the knockout round of the NBA Cup yet, and they're facing an uphill battle to get there this year thanks to their blowout loss to the Cavs. But they aren't dead yet, especially if they beat the Heat on Wednesday and the Cavs lose to the Knicks.

So, as you gather round your family for the holidays this week, be sure to tell them what really matters: Sweating NBA Cup games on Thanksgiving Eve.

#2709. Saudi national charged in OpenAI bugging to be released on $1 million bond

Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room, will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.

Alfozan, 35, is expected to be released on Wednesday and be fitted with an ankle monitor, according to his attorney, David Rizk.

"We are simply grateful that the Court made the right decision to release Mr. Alfozan and now we look forward to defending the case," Rizk said in a text message after a hearing before U.S. District Court Magistrate Judge Alex Tse.

Tse's ruled in favor of a release motion by Rizk filed last week that said prosecutors had admitted they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."

The San Francisco U.S. Attorney's Office opposed the former OpenAI engineer's release, saying in its response that he remains a flight risk due to his "considerable resources and ties to Saudi Arabia."

Alfozan's release request "blithely dismisses the seriousness" of the charge he faces, and "appears to presume, without support, that the government's investigation is complete," prosecutors wrote.

Alfozan, who was arrested Oct. 9, is accused of concealing a recording device in the artificial intelligence company's conference room to secretly record discussions. The device was found in a USB port in a television in the room on Sept. 30, prosecutors have said.

Prosecutors said they have found hundreds of audio files on Alfozan's personal computer, "includingdozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets."

Jury selection for Alfozan's trial is scheduled to begin Jan. 20.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm that lists Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of that nation's ruling royal family, as a fellow partner.

OpenAI did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Alfozan's planned release.

— CNBC's Ashley Capoot contributed to this article.

The Danish forward, who previously represented Manchester United, wasted no time in reminding the home supporters of his allegiances with a pointed celebration.

Hojlund could not resist taking a swipe at his former cross-town rivals during Tuesday's 2-2 draw in the Champions League. Reflecting on his recent performances, he explained: "I think I've been quite unlucky, a couple of chances where I could've done better, but also some with good goalkeeping from the opponents. For example, the Lazio goalkeeper made some incredible saves, I also remember against Venezia it was cleared off the line."

By continuing to find the net against top-tier opposition like City, he is slowly answering the doubters who questioned his transfer fee and output. As Napoli look to climb both the Serie A table and the European standings, the fitness and form of their primary striker will be paramount. With the injury crisis slowly abating, the Danish star believes the team is ready to surge back into contention for major honors.

"I want to keep positive, I scored lots of goals on international duty, I scored at San Siro, against Como, against City, so I need to keep improving, listen to my teammates and coach, and hope to score more goals," Hojlund concluded.

#2706. Drugmaker Insmed shares rally 14% after Sanofi agrees to buy it in pharma’s biggest deal in three years

Insmed shares rallied 14% on Tuesday after Sanofi announced it would buy the smaller drugmaker for $41 billion, marking the largest pharma deal since 2023.

The proposed acquisition includes Insmed's portfolio of two commercialized respiratory drugs as well as its pipeline of experimental candidates. Sanofi will pay $175 per share in cash.

It's a big bet by new Sanofi CEO Belen Garijo, who took up the role in May to steer the company through its upcoming patent cliff, when its blockbuster immunology drug Dupixent will face generic competition as patents expire.

If closed, the transaction will be the biggest one the industry has seen since Pfizer bought Seagen for $43 billion in December 2023.

Insmed shares closed up 14.1% at $170.15 on Tuesday, while Sanofi stock closed up 10.9% in Paris.

Barclays analysts called the deal a "breath of fresh air," significantly improving Sanofi's Dupixent patent expiry outlook by adding Insmed's established respiratory assets Brinsupri and Arikayce, plus a late-stage pipeline offering further potential upside.

The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2027 and be accretive to earnings by the end of 2028, Sanofi said.

Like much of the industry, Sanofi faces a revenue drop toward the end of the decade as top-selling drugs lose exclusivity and open up for generic drugmakers to compete.

Global biopharma has experienced a sharp uptick in deals in 2026, as the sector is driven by looming patent cliffs, newly buoyant public markets and a race to beef up pipelines. Following a post-pandemic trough in 2022, M&A activity surged in 2025 and has accelerated into 2026.

While companies develop new medicines in-house, acquiring them is often faster and has become the go-to strategy for large biopharma companies with strong cash flows and financial firepower. Investors are also calling for sustainable revenue growth as major drugs lose exclusivity.

"Sanofi could offer a far more attractive investment opportunity given the significantly improved growth outlook," Barclays said in a note early Tuesday.

Formerly CEO at Merck KGaA, Garijo is facing pressure to find Sanofi's next growth driver. Her predecessor Paul Hudson stepped down earlier this year after the board chose not to renew his mandate, following a six-year reign during which his costly emphasis on R&D failed to deliver speedy results.

The Insmed deal is a good strategic fit that will help soften the blow from Dupixent's expected 2031–32 loss of exclusivity, said Jefferies analysts.

Insmed's medicines Brinsupri and Arikayce could add significant long-term revenue, with forecasts of $6.8 billion and $1.3 billion, respectively, in 2035, the analysts said. They see further potential upside if a settlement delays Dupixent's patent cliff, or if Brinsupri is included in Sanofi's alliance with Regeneron and positioned alongside Dupixent in respiratory diseases.

"The deal does not fix Sanofi's R&D/pipeline questions, but it buys time," the analysts said, adding that one potential wrinkle is future competition from AstraZeneca's tozorakimab, which is being studied for treatment in bronchiectasis, a chronic lung condition.

"Insmed builds on our already successful presence in respiratory diseases through its highly differentiated portfolio, strong science and proven commercial success," CEO Garijo said in a statement.

Sanofi shares fell 2.9%, and Insmed rose 24% on Nov. 19 after Bloomberg reported that a deal was underway.

#2705. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2704. Dell earnings beat on AI strength, company authorizes $25 billion buyback

Dell reported third-quarter earnings on Tuesday that topped Wall Street expectations for earnings and sales, and provided a strong guide for the fourth quarter.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

Dell said that it expected earnings of $8.40 per share on revenue of about $56.3 billion in the current quarter. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for $7.50 per share on $53.61 billion in revenue.

The company's overall sales more than doubled on an annual basis during the third quarter, driven by AI server demand.

The company's largest line is its infrastructure solutions group, or ISG, which makes AI servers and systems, often based around AI chips from companies like Nvidia and AMD.

ISG sales during the quarter were $41.97 billion, topping an LSEG estimate of $35.27 billion. Of that, $22 billion was for AI-optimized servers, up 291% on an annual basis. ISG now accounts for 85% of Dell's total revenue.

"We are now a different company, with Infrastructure Solutions Group driving the vast majority of our profit and growth," said Jeff Clarke, Dell's vice chairman and COO, in a statement. He said that Dell has 7,500 customers buying AI servers.

In prepared remarks, Clarke said that Dell has a competitive advantage over other computer equipment makers because it allows customers to get their computers working within hours of delivery.

"That allows customers to convert backlog into revenue sooner and more reliably," Clarke said in the prepared remarks published by Dell.

The company also raised its forecast for AI-servers in the coming fiscal year to $78 billion, versus $74 billion expected. The company says it has a backlog of $132.3 billion in AI server orders it is working through.

"Every AI order is vetted for access to capital, end customer quality, trade and export compliance and access to land and power," Clarke said on an earnings call with analysts.

The company also sells laptops and PCs, in a group it calls CSG, or client services group. It reported $12.89 billion in sales, which was below LSEG expectations of $14.33 billion.

The overall PC market has been getting whacked of late, with some estimates calling the lull the worst ever, as rising memory prices have led to higher device prices and consumers are choosing to hang on to their old laptops longer.

Dell said that it had authorized $25 billion in share repurchases, and that it had spent $4.9 billion in the third quarter on share repurchases and dividends.

Dell shares are up over 135% so far in 2026.

#2703. No. 13 St. John's beats Oregon 90-79 in opening round of Players Era 16 tournament

LAS VEGAS (AP) — Ian Jackson scored 21 points as part of a balanced effort by No. 13 St. John's, which beat Oregon 90-79 on Tuesday night in the opening round of the Players Era 16 tournament.

Keyshawn Hall reached 2,000 career points by adding 17 for the Red Storm (6-0), who put five players in double figures. Junior forward Ruben Prey scored a career-high 15, and Tounde Yessoufou had 14.

St. John's made only three 3-pointers but shot 53% from the field and sank 25 of 27 free throws (93%).

Jerry Easter II had 14 points off the bench to lead Oregon (3-2), which has lost back-to-back games. The Ducks missed their first 11 attempts from 3-point distance and finished 3 of 15 (20%).

St. John's took Oregon out of its game early. The Ducks entered averaging 24.3 assists, but had only nine against the Red Storm. Johnnies point guard Quinn Ellis produced eight assists by himself, coming close to a triple-double by also totaling 14 points and eight rebounds.

It was the first meeting between the programs since St. John's defeated the Ducks 61-51 on Jan. 29, 1977. Oregon won the only other matchup in the 1975 NIT third-place game.

The Red Storm closed the first half on a 10-2 run to take a 45-30 lead into the locker room, its pressure defense holding the Ducks without a field goal over the final 4:10.

That late spurt appeared to take any drama out of the game, and St. John's led by as many as 22. The Red Storm entered outscoring opponents by nearly 30 points per game, but Oregon made a little bit of a late charge, twice cutting the deficit to eight in the final two minutes and shooting 56.3% in the second half.

St. John's plays No. 25 Louisville on Thursday night, pitting Red Storm coach Rick Pitino against his former team.

Oregon faces No. 5 Texas Tech in a consolation game Thursday night.

Key Matchups Jayson Tatum vs Anthony DavisDavis is averaging 25.1 points, 9.9 rebounds, 2.0 assists, 1.2 steals and 1.1 blocks per game. Honorable MentionHugo Gonzalez vs AJ DybantsaDybantsa, the #1 pick in the draft, is averaging 16.5 points, 6.9 rebounds, 2.8 assists, and 1.3 steals per game. He is shooting 42.3% from the field and 23.5% from beyond the arc. The Celtics need to defend him well as he is capable of having a big game. He is also a good rebounder and the Celtics need to keep him off the boards. Hugo may or may not start here.

Keys to the GameDefense – Defense is always the key to winning games. The Celtics are 2nd in the league with a defensive rating of 105.3 The Wizards are 20th with a defensive rating of 115.3. Although the Wizards have an offensive rating of 110.3 (26th), they can get hot at any time if they are not being closely defended. In spite of being 2nd in defense, the Celtics too often slack off in games and allow way too many easy shots. They need to commit to playing lock down defense for 48 minutes with no let up.

Rebound – Rebounding is also a key to winning every game. The Wizards are 19th with 42.7 rebounds per game while the Celtics are 10th with 45.2 rebounds per game. They simply have to fight harder to grab rebounds than the Wizards. The Celtics started the season as one of the best rebounding teams in the league but have slipped in recent games. They are 19th with 31.2 defensive rebounds and 28th in the 4th quarter for defensive rebounds. They allowed the Warriors 19 offensive rebounds, with 5 of them in the 4th quarter, which the Warriors turned into 22 second chance points. The Celtics must put out extra effort to crash the boards in this, and every, game to get back to winning. Move the Ball Carefully – The Celtics are at their best when the ball moves and they make the extra pass. When the ball sticks and players try to do too much, the Celtics struggle. But they have to make careful passes and not get sloppy because the Wizards average 15.8 points off turnovers and 12.4 fast break points per game. The Celtics need to be careful with their passes and ball handling because if they aren't the Wizards will make them pay.

Focus on Making Shots – The Celtics have been struggling to score in recent games, especially from the perimeter. Against the Nuggets they shot 40.8% from the field and 25.0% from three. Against the Kings they shot 33.7% from the field and 22.4% on threes. Against the Warriors, they shot 38.6% from the field and 29.8% from beyond the arc. It may be fatigue or the travel or just a lack of focus, but they have to get their focus back on taking good shots and making them. They are struggling especially on threes but yet they keep shooting, and missing, them. If they continue to shoot 20% on 3's, they need to focus on getting into the paint and not keep shooting bricks from outside.

X-FactorsOn the Road and Fatigue – The Celtics are playing in the final game of a 4 game road trip. The last game of a road trip is always hard to win. They are also playing in the 3rd game in 4 nights so fatigue may come into play. They have to overcome the distractions that come with playing on the road like travel, missing family, an unfamiliar building and a hostile crowd. They have to stay focused on playing Celtics basketball the right way and not on the crowd, the court, or the Wizards' record.

Officiating -Officiating is always an x-factor in every game. Every crew officiates differently. Some call it tight, others let them play. Some favor the home team. Some favor the road team. Some just make bad, head scratching calls. The Celtics need to adjust to how the refs are calling the game and not allow bad calls or no calls to take away their focus on playing the game.

#2701. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: AI race spreads from robots to smartphones

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

I'm just back from APEC in Shenzhen, where I saw the leaders of the U.S., China and Russia gather along with other member economies. Beneath the fanfare, what's changed on the tech front in what's often dubbed China's Silicon Valley?

It only took a few months for a humanoid robot to get better at picking up household items.

Even after a human knocked a box and toys away from their original spots, a LimX robot quickly located them and moved them to pre-set locations. While still far from perfect, the smoothness of the demo contrasted with the robot's more stilted movements I'd seen early this summer — and inability then to react to rapid changes in tasks.

When we caught up in Shenzhen last week, CFO Jesse Liu declined to comment on LimX's IPO plans. But he told me that his conversations around the APEC CEO Summit revealed an excitement around a new development for humanoids: growing demand for robot chips that can run smarter large language models locally, instead of on the cloud.

"A lot of chipmakers are now happy about the latest developments in LLMs because they think robotic OEMs will actually begin to integrate more storage, and reasoning, and compute, and inference infrastructure on the edge, or locally inside the robot body," Liu said, referring to original equipment manufacturers.

"They do see robots moving from hundreds of units to thousands of units, to tens of thousands of units, beginning to enter that kind of automotive quantity," he said, pointing to chip suppliers such as Horizon Robotics.

New AI models, particularly GPT-6 Astra, have shown not just improved reasoning, but better control of robots.

That puts more of the onus on hardware makers, for robots or other devices, to create practical applications. LimX said it's working with business partners in over 30 markets, including Switzerland where the robots serve as power plant inspectors in rural areas.

The key for powering AI's transition into the physical world is on-device chips and more powerful batteries. That means Nvidia's most expensive, cutting-edge chips won't be the only ones powering generative AI models.

So far, even in basic consumer applications such as smartphones, AI isn't transformative enough to be a top-three selling point for mass-market users, Thomas Bai, AI product expert at smartphone company Honor, told me in Shenzhen.

He expects that in the next few years, as smartphone chips are able to run more powerful AI models, the "super agent" tech can become better at completing tasks on its own, and anticipating a user's needs — such as ordering coffee.

Counterpoint on Nov. 19 forecast that in the retail industry, "edge AI" revenue will surge sixfold by 2032, with roughly half of revenue coming from retail robots that clean and check inventory — often with the help of smaller AI models that can run on locally embedded chips.

But more capable, on-device AI also requires more battery power, Honor's Bai pointed out.

Honor's X70 Android smartphone, which launched last summer, boasts a battery with 8,300 milliampere-hours for a total device weight of just under 200 grams. That compares with 5,000 mAH for the 214-gram flagship Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra, and roughly 5,400 mAH for this year's 249-gram Apple iPhone 18 Pro Max, according to GSM Arena.

LimX's Oli humanoid comes with 9,500 mAH battery capacity, lasting the robot about 1.5 hours.

Such AI applications, however, remain sensitive to the memory-chip shortage weighing on the industry.

The X70 was the best-selling Android phone in the entry to mid-tier range, said Shenzhen-based Counterpoint analyst Shiwen Ma.

While Ma said penetration of smartphones with generative AI features is higher in China at 55% versus 38% overseas, the offerings are still not essential to users, while rising memory-chip costs make it harder for manufacturers to bring AI-powered devices to the mass market.

Geely-backed automotive tech company EcarX's CFO Dylan Jeng told me earlier this month he expects the memory price surge to persist through 2027, and even the first half of 2028.

The chipmaker that can narrow the gap for on-device AI needs could find its moment soon.

Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke exclusively on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week.

How China is using summits to expand its global influence

For a second year in a row, Xi has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores. But U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returned from Asia.

Putin scores 'massive win' at first trilateral meeting

The first-ever three-way summit is "arguably a diplomatic victory for Moscow," said Andrius Tursa, an advisor for Central and Eastern Europe and Russia at Teneo. The view from inside the APEC welcome banquet showed the leaders were in close proximity from the start of the summit.

#2699. ‘It’s not normal’ – Stephen A. Smith says what recently happened to Joe Burrow is an ‘aberration’

The Joe Burrow MVP campaign recently hit a roadblock with a loss to the Washington Commanders, and Stephen A. Smith noticed an 'aberration'.

Coming into the game against the Washington Commanders, Joe Burrow's Cincinnati Bengals were the clear favourites.

Not only did the Bengals lose, but Burrow's counterpart, Jayden Daniels, outperformed him to put a huge dent in his MVP campaign.

Stephen A. Smith noticed something "not normal" with Burrow that he's convinced won't happen again.

Burrow was putting up some truly historic stats coming into the Commanders game this season, so the law of averages was bound to come up at some point.

Unfortunately for him, it came up in a game his team were clear favourites for, and he couldn't do anything to prevent it.

Common sense dictates that the historic stats he was putting up before this game weren't sustainable, but neither is the poor game against the Commanders.

Stephen A. Smith is convinced of the latter, which is why he picked out some stats of Burrow from the game and said it's "not normal" what happened to him.

Speaking on ESPN, he said: "The flip side to this is that you lose 22 to 20. And how many people are going to be able to hold Joe Burrow in that offence to 20 points? It's an aberration. It's not normal."

Smith added that Burrow will bounce back, and his team will prepare him better to counter defences like the one he faced against the Commanders.

Ultimately, Burrow has proven beyond doubt that he's an elite quarterback, so this game was a blip more than a judgement on his abilities.

The Bengals still remain the favourites for the playoffs, and Burrow will play a huge role in what happens with them once they get there.

In fact, this loss against the Commanders might end up helping them in the long run because now they know a type of defence they struggle against, allowing them to plan better in the future when more teams repeat this tactic.

#2698. Report: NFL, YouTube are negotiating a three-game package for 2027

YouTube's on-again, off-again arrangements to stream select NFL games may be on again.

John Ourand of Puck reports in his Varsity newsletter that the NFL and YouTube have been negotiating a three-game package for the 2027 season. The arrangement would include a Christmas Eve game, the Week 17 Saturday game to be televised by NBC, and the Week 10 European game that was televised by Fox.

This year, Christmas Eve lands on a Thursday; Prime Video will televise the Texans-Eagles game that night (barring a flex). Next year, Christmas Eve lands on a Friday.

YouTube was at one point negotiating the five-game package for 2026 that landed with Netflix. It includes Wednesday night's first-of-its-kind Thanksgiving Eve game between the Packers and Rams. YouTube walked, as Ourand reported at the time, when the league tried to split the five-game package between multiple partners.

The arrangement between the NFL and YouTube currently includes Sunday Ticket. That package could be disappearing in the next round of broadcast deals.

The inevitable expansion of the regular season to 18 games per team will create another 16 games that can be sliced and diced into special packages.

#2697. China has changed tactics but is still thwarting India's rise, a U.S. panel says

China is "actively working to suppress India's rise," even as the relationship between the two major Asian powers has thawed in the past year, according to a U.S. panel that sees an opening for greater outreach to India.

On Nov. 18, a U.S. China Economic and Security Review Commission report said Beijing is applying military, economic, maritime, and other forms of pressure to constrain India.

China has recalled engineers and withheld equipment to slow India's electronics manufacturing, slashed pharmaceutical input prices to undercut new Indian plants, and imposed export controls on battery technology to impede the development of India's clean energy industry, the report said.

It added that these tensions offer the U.S. an opportunity to counter growing Chinese influence by building closer ties with India across key sectors such as pharmaceutical and defense.

Ties have improved since a deadly border skirmish in 2020, but experts told CNBC that trust is still thin between Beijing and New Delhi. However, they added that the uneasy peace with China is unlikely to make India align automatically with the U.S. as an ally.

The Indian foreign and commerce ministries and the Chinese foreign ministry did not respond to emails seeking comments.

The thawing in relations between India and China came amid industrial demand, as well as the need for "a whole lot of posturing for the U.S.," said Arpit Chaturvedi, South Asia advisor at strategic advisory firm Teneo.

Over the past year, India faced tariff pressures and needed to signal to Washington that its options remain open, including that of renegotiating relations with China, he explained, but added that as the trade deal with the U.S. is "close enough now," there is no pressing need for that signaling.

Further improvement in the relationship is also limited by reports of renewed border tensions between the two sides and Beijing's scant reciprocation on easing access to technology and people movement, he said.

Last month, an Indian military official said that confrontations with Chinese soldiers were ongoing across several locations along the country's northeastern border, even as top leaders from the countries met with fanfare at the BRICS Summit in September.

Another flash point was when Beijing put export controls on tunnel boring machines last month. "Our relations with China remain good, and if there aren't any new hurdles, they can improve further," India's Commerce Minister Piyush Goyal reportedly said after China's move to tighten TBM exports.

On Monday, a Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson reportedly said Beijing is ready to work with India to facilitate visa arrangements for business personnel from both sides amid reports that fewer Indian businessmen got visas to attend an annual trade fair in Shanghai.

The Chinese "actively disrupt" areas where India is trying to make progress in terms of moving global supply chains, developing advanced industrial technologies such as EVs, batteries, electronic components, and pharmaceutical ingredients, Pramit Pal Chaudhuri, South Asia practice head at Eurasia Group, told CNBC.

He added China is increasingly focused on containing India economically rather than through military overtures.

India's manufacturing push still relies on Chinese inputs, from electronics components and solar materials to rare earth magnets and specialized machinery. Between April and October, India imported goods worth $96.6 billion from China, up more than 30% from a year ago, as per commerce ministry data released earlier this month.

Its major imports from China include electronic goods, machinery, chemicals, transport equipment, iron and steel, and pharmaceutical products.

While India needs inputs for its manufacturing ambitions from Beijing and China values access to Indian markets, trust between the two sides is "still thin," according to Reema Bhattacharya, head of Asia research at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft, citing a large trade imbalance and overlapping interests in South Asia and the Indian Ocean as additional issues.

#2696. Joel Embiid doubtful for Sixers’ NBA Cup showdown vs. Miami Heat

The Sixers are getting dangerously close to full strength, which means in true Sixers fashion, calamity is right around the corner. Lo and behold, Joel Embiid came down with an illness that caused him to miss Monday's game against the Portland Trail Blazers, and he's still dealing with it a day later.

Embiid is doubtful to suit up for the Sixers' NBA Cup game against the Miami Heat on Wednesday, according to the NBA's injury report. The good news is that aside from Embiid and Ariel Hukporti (Achilles), no one else is even listed for the Sixers. So much for Jaylen Brown's hamstring injury!

On Miami's side, two-way signees Tre Donaldson and Vladislav Goldin are out on assignment in the G League, while Myron Gardner is available. The big question mark is Giannis Antetokounmpo, who's listed as questionable with right groin tendinitis. He missed Miami's win over the Oklahoma City Thunder on Sunday with the same injury, and in his absence, Bam Adebayo messed around and got a triple-double.

If Giannis is able to suit up and Embiid misses the game, the Sixers will have a hell of a time stopping him and Bam down low. None of Jabari Walker, Dominick Barlow or Adem Bona have the size to combat the Greek Freak in transition. But if Giannis does sit out, it'll be more of a fair fight.

The Heat sit atop East Group B with a 2-0 record, while the Sixers are 1-1. If the Heat beat the Sixers, they'll be all but guaranteed to win the group, as they'll have the head-to-head tiebreaker over both the Sixers and Cleveland Cavaliers. But if the Embiid-less Sixers can take down the Heat, they'll have a chance to win Group B as long as the New York Knicks beat the Cavs later Wednesday.

The Sixers have never advanced to the knockout stage of the NBA Cup, but they've also never had NBA Cup MVP LeBron James on their roster before this year. And since LeBron is playing on a veteran-minimum contract this year, the prize money for winning the Cup would be more than 10 percent of his salary. That's assuredly a huge motivation for someone with an estimated net worth of $1.4 billion.

Embiid's likely absence will give the Sixers a chance to continue experimenting with their backup options as they inch closer to the start of unofficial trade season. They largely went with a Walker-Barlow combo against the Blazers—Bona played only five minutes in his first game back from a cervical spine sprain—but it's worth keeping an eye on whether the Sixers change that up against the Heat. They should still be in the intel-gathering stage of the season, so the more they mix it up, the better. (Could we even get some brief Tacko Time?)

As someone whose two-year-old is just getting over hand, foot and mouth disease, it's that time of the year for parents. Here's hoping the rest of the Sixers stay healthy and Embiid feels better soon.

#2694. China’s Xiamen Airlines to buy 50 Boeing jets in $7.4 billion deal

China Southern Airlines said in a filing late Tuesday that its subsidiary Xiamen Airlines had agreed to purchase 50 Boeing 737-8 aircraft with a combined catalog price of approximately $7.4 billion.

The announcement comes after President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he expected China to purchase another 250 Boeing aircraft, following his meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping at the APEC summit last week.

"By the way, I sold 250 Boeings to China, and I think they're going to do another 250," Trump said.

In May, China approved an initial purchase of 200 Boeing aircraft for Chinese airlines following Trump's visit to Beijing, marking the country's first commitment to purchase U.S.-made Boeing aircraft since 2017, according to the White House.

It was not immediately clear whether Xiamen Airlines' latest purchase was part of that commitment.

China Southern, which owns a 55% stake in Xiamen Airlines, said the actual purchase price would be lower than the combined list price because of discounts offered by Boeing. The company did not disclose the final purchase price, citing confidentiality provisions in the agreement.

The aircraft are scheduled to be delivered in batches between 2031 and 2034, with the purchase financed through a combination of internal funds and external financing, the filing showed. The transaction remains subject to shareholder and relevant Chinese government approvals.

China Southern said the purchase would support Xiamen Airlines' planned relocation to the new Xiamen Xiang'an International Airport and the expansion of its route network.

The agreement follows the group's recent purchase agreement for 137 Airbus A320neo-family aircraft, the filing showed. China Southern said sourcing aircraft from different manufacturers would help diversify its fleet and improve operational flexibility.

Other Asian carriers, including Philippine Airlines and Malaysia Aviation Group, have also announced Boeing aircraft orders this month.

#2692. Knicks’ Josh Hart is the cover athlete of SLAM 265

After an off-season full of celebrations and media appearances, the Knicks are back to being fully concentrated on their pursuit of another championship. Their fans aren't far behind them as they've reverted to dissecting every game as if it was game seven of the NBA Finals. Or better yet, life and death. But that hasn't stopped the media from capitalizing on the post championship hype surrounding the Knicks.

There's still plenty of odd articles and television segments covering the Knicks, their owner, their slow start, and extension-less stars. Jalen Brunson is just weeks removed from a Law & Order cameo. And reports surfaced earlier this week that the defending champions have been the most watched NBA team this season. But it won't end there.

It was announced on Tuesday morning that Josh Hart will be featured on the cover of the upcoming SLAM magazine issue. This is now the fifth time a Knick has been on the cover of the publication since May. OG Anunoby was the latest to dawn the cover in SLAM 263, with Brunson being featured on the cover of the Knicks' championship issue. And just a few weeks before that, the Knicks were on the cover of back-to-back issues featuring both the Eastern Conference Championship and Anunoby's game-winning tip-in.

"We sat down with JHart to talk winning, the sacrifices that come with it, putting your teammates first and what it really takes to be a champion," SLAM said.

You can check out the 10-minute video of his interview here, and read the cover story, titled The People's Champ here.

#2691. Six key questions raised by Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia

Jensen Huang made a dramatic return as Nvidia CEO on Monday, less than a week after board Chairman Harvey Jones said directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back.

Nvidia shares surged as much as 36% during the session and closed 31% higher.

The reversal followed a steep decline in Nvidia shares, which closed at $125.53 on Friday even after the company reported strong third-quarter results. A petition calling for Huang's return had drawn more than 200,000 signatures, according to its organizers.

The rally spread across the broader market, with the Nasdaq Composite gaining 6.5% and the S&P 500 rising 4.7%, each posting its strongest session since April 2025.

Nvidia's board said it had received input from senior U.S. government officials and that its investigation into Huang's conduct would continue.

Still, the board's reversal, President Donald Trump's administration's role and continued scrutiny of Nvidia's China business leave major questions for both investors and lawmakers.

Sen. Jack Reed, a member of the Senate Banking Committee, urged Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Monday to appear before the committee to discuss the case.

Reed did not lay out a specific list of questions for the two officials, but his statement cited reporting that Trump had been briefed by intelligence officials led by then-National Security Adviser Marco Rubio before intervening in support of Huang's return.

Reed's call adds to mounting pressure on both Nvidia and the Trump administration to explain what they knew and when.

Rep. Gabe Amo, D-R.I., vice ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said Huang’s reinstatement could broaden congressional scrutiny rather than bring it to a close.

“I think it frankly broadens the scope of the query,” Amo told CNBC, adding that lawmakers should also examine administration officials involved in decisions affecting Nvidia and chip sales to China.

Amo said he expects the planned closed-door interview with Huang on Dec. 1 to proceed and that Congress would continue seeking answers both privately and publicly in the coming months.

“He should start purchasing his plane tickets to Washington,” he said. “It is abundantly clear that this is ripe for questioning by Congress.”

On Nvidia's third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 17, Jones said the board had accepted Huang's resignation and did not intend to reinstate him.

Less than a week later, the board reversed course, saying Monday that it had unanimously voted to bring Huang back after discussions with him and input from senior U.S. government officials.

Trump said he had spoken with board members and that his administration provided assurances based on the information available to it. But the board has not publicly detailed what it was told.

"The board decided to do what we wanted to do," Trump told CNBC, adding that he believed his intervention had been important.

"I've been speaking to the board directly and indirectly, and I felt very strongly about it," he said.

Trump also called into an Nvidia board meeting to argue for Huang's return, Semafor reported, citing people familiar with the matter.

Reed has called on Huang to disclose his communications with Trump and on Nvidia to release a transcript of the board's call with the president.

Lawmakers are still seeking answers about the alleged smuggling of Nvidia products to China, their possible diversion for military use and possible advancement of Chinese AI capabilities.

The Senate Banking Committee has scheduled Huang to testify on U.S. export controls on Dec. 3. House lawmakers have also confirmed closed-door interviews with Huang on Dec. 1 and Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter on Dec. 2.

"The Nvidia board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," Reps. John Moolenaar, Rick Crawford and Brian Mast said in a joint statement.

Their inquiry may also examine what Nvidia knew about the customers and resellers involved, as well as what controls the company had in place.

Other lawmakers have also sought to preserve the paper trail. Sens. Elizabeth Warren and Chris Coons had asked senior administration officials on Nov. 11 to retain records related to Nvidia exports of advanced AI chips to China and associated national security concerns.

Nvidia has promised to hire a chief compliance officer and establish a committee overseeing national security and export controls. The board's investigation into Huang's conduct also remains underway.

Josh Gilbert, eToro's lead analyst for APAC and the Middle East, said investors will be watching whether those new safeguards carry meaningful authority.

Gil Luria, head of technology research at D.A. Davidson, said Nvidia has a limited window to prepare for the possibility that a future president may be less supportive of Huang.

With Trump's term ending in two years, Luria argued that Nvidia needs a clearer plan for who could take over if Huang were forced to leave again.

Gilbert pointed to Nvidia's roughly 75% revenue-growth forecast for the next financial year as evidence of strong near-term demand. The longer-term question, he said, is whether customer orders and artificial intelligence investment can sustain that pace.

Luria told CNBC that concerns about the broader AI trade remain, including the cost of financing data centers, potential overbuilding by neocloud companies and obstacles to building new facilities.

Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon said worries about whether AI spending is achievable and sustainable were not caused solely by Huang's departure. UBS, meanwhile, flagged Nvidia's roughly $390 billion in supply commitments and the need to keep demand flowing through its ecosystem.

Gilbert said part of Monday's rally reflected investors recovering earlier losses rather than pricing in new growth. Bernstein restored its "Outperform" rating and kept its $200 price target, while UBS maintained its $220 target.

"For the share price, it comes down to whether Nvidia can keep delivering as it has done over recent years," said Gilbert, adding that Huang's return could ease some concerns about Nvidia's longer-term outlook.

"The real worry has been what happens beyond next year. Having Huang back at the helm goes a long way to easing those concerns, because he's the one who sits down with the biggest customers and gets those multi-year orders over the line," he said.

— CNBC's David Sucherman, Justin Papp and Eko Prasetyo contributed to the story

#2690. 49ers safety gets last laugh against Seattle ahead of crucial NFC West showdown

49ers safety Malik Mustapha is saving money.

On Tuesday, NFL insider Adam Schefter revealed that Mustapha, who appealed his fine from the league, won his case for a hit he made against Seahawks wide receiver Jaxon Smith-Njigba.

Malik Mustapha celebrating in an NFL game against the Vikings last Sunday. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Mustapha's appeal was overseen by former Packers wide receiver Jordy Nelson, who served as the hearing officer, and the 49ers player will save $6,998.

Mustapha was running toward Smith-Njigba on a pass play. Once the ball hit Smith-Njigba's hands, the two collided — resulting in the ball popping out of the reigning Offensive Player of the Year's hands for an incompletion.

Mustapha rose to his feet quickly after the play, while Smith-Njigba rolled over on his stomach area and momentarily remained on the ground.

While Mustapha hopes to put the play behind him, the Seahawks likely haven't forgotten about it ahead of Sunday's game.

Seahawks wide receiver Jaxon Smith-Njigba running with the ball during an NFL game. Getty Images

When the teams met in Seattle earlier this season, the 49ers put together a 32-29 statement victory to not only forget about last season's 41-6 playoff loss to the Seahawks but also propel them to the top of the NFC West.

Quarterback Brock Purdy was 19-of-29 passing for 189 yards with two touchdowns and one interception that put him in the MVP conversation. Purdy executed a 12-play drive in the first half and completed 6 of 7 passes — four of which went to Mike Evans for 75 yards.

49ers defense celebrating in an NFL game against the Dallas Cowboys. AP Photo/Tony Gutierrez

Purdy's heroics from the first half carried into the second. The Niners executed 13 plays on a drive that started in the third and carried into the fourth quarter. San Francisco capped it with a 7-yard touchdown reception to Brandin Cooks and followed it with a two-point conversion to Evans.

Seattle tied the game at 29 with 6:59 left, but San Francisco answered with a field goal. The 49ers' defense held the Seahawks to only five plays before it forced a turnover on downs, and San Francisco ran out the clock.

The Niners (9-1) not only have a chance to solidify themselves as the NFC West's best team but also have an opportunity to move further ahead in the NFC standings.

Seattle (6-4), the defending Super Bowl champion, is hoping a victory would keep it in the wild-card race.

#2689. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2688. The “Julian Champagnie for Three-Point Contest” campaign begins here

Having the Spurs well represented at All-Star Weekend the last few years has been a new feeling, and I'm not just talking about the Rising Stars Challenge or the main event. Stephon Castle and Carter Bryant came in as runners up in the Dunk Contest the last two years, marking San Antonio's first representation there since the 1980's. Victor Wembanyama also participated in the Skills Challenge in both his rookie and sophomore campaigns (to not-so-great results, as Anthony Edwards' horsing around cost Team Top Pick the first time, then Wemby and Chris Paul's attempts to cheese it got them disqualified in the second go-round.)

But one event the Spurs have always felt underrepresented at is the Three-Point Contest. Despite being one of the teams that helped to revolutionize the three-pointer and often having some of the top shooters in the league, their history there is limited to Steve Smith (2002), Roger Mason (2009), Matt Bonner (2013) and Marco Belinelli (2014 winner, 2015). And Bonner basically had to plead and even campaign for himself to get in.

The Spurs have had many more deserving participants than that, but it's no lie that the league likes to go for trendier names since these events suffer enough for viewership as it is, and this is an event superstars can participate in without fear of injury or overexertion. Perhaps the most obvious gimmick ever was allowing Damian Lillard to participate last season despite not playing in a single game while recovering from a ruptured Achilles. Was he medically cleared to shoot threes? Of course. Was it a feel-good story? It was, but was it fair to perhaps another deserving participant who played the whole season and otherwise missed out on some bonus money? That is the ultimate question, and I can easily name one such player: Julian Champagnie.

Champagnie is the ultimate feel-good story himself. Undrafted out of St. Johns, he signed a two-way contract with the 76ers in 2022, just to be waived for Mac McClung, whom they only wanted to represent them at the Dunk Contest before he was waived himself. Their loss was the Spurs' gain, and they quickly grabbed him to help with their lack of shooters, and he's been filling that gap beautifully ever since.

Whether starting or coming off the bench, he has always been one of the steadier hands in the NBA. Even though he initially began last season off the bench, he kept finding himself filling in for injured starters before eventually taking over the starting power forward position for good, after Harrison Barnes fell into a slump he couldn't escape, and it became obvious that having Champangie out there either took defensive pressure off Wemby, or defenders forgot about him and left him wide open. More often than not, he made them pay by hitting 38.1% on over six attempts per game. That shooting carried over into the playoffs, where he hit 39.6% of his threes as a starter for a team that made the finals.

This year has been even more of the same in terms of being in and out of the starting lineup, except he's shooting even better. While it's still early, he's currently shooting 41.7% on 7 attempts per game, both career highs. Because the season is still young, that "only" places him at 38th in the league in three-point percentage, but amongplayers who have played in the majority of games and are shooting at least 7 attempts per game, that places him behind only Desmond Bane, Duncan Robinson, Klay Thompson, Steph Curry, Grayson Allen and Jamal Murray. That's six players, and the contest has eight participants. Odds are as the season progresses, the real shooters of the league will begin to separate themselves, and Champagnie will be one of them.

With the Spurs having all the eyes on them now, surely the league will want them well represented at All-Star Weekend. While Wemby is a shoo-in for the All-Star game itself, no one else really is, so maybe putting their best shooter in the Three-Point Contest will be a good start. If Champagnie keeps this up, he'll be more than deserving, and hopefully it won't take a Matt Bonner campaign to get it done, but in case it does, we're starting it right here.

Today is November 24, 2026, and with the zero power I have, I am officially nominating Julian Champagnie for the 2027 Three-Point Contest in the Footprint Center in Phoenix, Arizona on Feburary 20. Who's with me?

Dinsmore, who lives in the U.K., was sent to the hospital after he experienced pain at a London event, TMZ reported.

Nick "Eugene" Dinsmore Nick 'Eugene' Dinsmore/Facebook

According to PW Insider, the 50-year-old traveled back to the U.K. on Monday night. Details about his current condition and whether he is still on a ventilator are unknown.

Dinsmore got his break in WWE as the beloved "Eugene," who was then-RAW general manager Eric Bischoff's child-like nephew, in 2004. The character's energy and "overly excited" innocence while also being a wrestling prodigy who grappled with anyone in the ring, got him over with the wrestling audience.

Eugene enters the ring during Monday Night RAW at Madison Square Garden in New York on Oct. 4, 2004. WWE via Getty Images

He went on to have matches with Triple H at SummerSlam 2004, Kurt Angle in 2005 and William Regal, with whom he won the WWF Tag Team championship in 2004. Dinsmore also shaved Bischoff's head bald during Taboo Tuesday in 2004.

He lasted in WWE until 2007, with his last appearance coming on the Aug. 31 SmackDown, where he passed out in a bearhug by Mark Henry after shooting the World's Strongest Man in the face with a T-shirt gun.

Eugene enters the ring during Monday Night RAW at Pepsi Arena in Albany, New York, June 7, 2004. WWE via Getty Images

Dinsmore began wrestling for Ohio Valley Wrestling in 1996, even becoming the WWE-linked promotion's world champion 10 times.

After leaving WWE, he spent time in Juggalo Championship Wrestling, OVW and numerous other independent promotions.

He served as an NXT trainer from 2013-14. Dinsmore started Midwest All Pro Wrestling in Sioux Falls, South Dakota, in 2015 before selling in 2021.

#2686. Paulson Adebo gets shot at Giants redemption after up-and-down season

Paulson Adebo looked out of place at practice last week as the NFL's highest-paid "don't-forget-about-me" trying to find his place on the punt team.

Two days later, Adebo improbably looked totally at home as a shutdown cornerback whose wild ride this season is about to accelerate further because he has risen from purgatory to again be considered one of the Giants' most important players.

"Obviously, it's not where I wanted to be going into the season," Adebo said after Tuesday's meetings. "You prepare every week like you're going to play. If you're the third [cornerback], you're one play away from going in every game, so you owe it to yourself and you owe it to your teammates to be prepared."

Adebo, whose $24.1 million salary cap charge is the highest on the Giants, lost his starting job to Deonte Banks and Greg Newsome II in training camp. He was picked on off the bench in Week 1, missed the next five games due to injury, returned to play well against the Texans as Banks' fill-in, was a healthy scratch against the Commanders and then saved the day with four passes defended and an interception after Newsome was injured against the Jaguars.

With Newsome now expected to miss four to six weeks due to ankle surgery, Adebo will start opposite Banks on Sunday against the Colts. The Giants will face three of the NFL's top six passing attacks in that six-game window.

New York Giants cornerback Paulson Adebo (21) runs off the field after an interception against the Jacksonville Jaguars in East Rutherford, N.J., Nov. 22, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"I know what type of player I am," Adebo said. "I know what I can do on the field. I know what type of person I am, so I don't think it's anything new necessarily that I've learned about myself, but certainly just staying prepared and ready to go when you're called upon."

Only 20 months after signing a three-year, $54 million contract as the prize of the Giants' 2025 free agent class, Adebo was inactive against the Commanders because backup cornerbacks Colton Hood and Nic Jones offered more special teams value. Adebo played 13 total special teams snaps from 2022-25 as he developed into a starting corner for the Saints and then the Giants' No. 1 option against elite receivers.

Head coach John Harbaugh pulled aside Adebo last week to keep him engaged with a message that turned out to be prescient.

New York Giants' Greg Newsome II (20) runs during training camp at the Greenbrier in White Sulphur Springs, W.Va., Saturday, Aug. 1, 2026. Corey Sipkin for the NY POST

"I said, 'In my mind, you're a starter in this league. And when you started here, you've done really well. And you're going to have to start again,' " Harbaugh said Nov. 18. "So when that time comes, he's in a good place."

The time came Sunday when Adebo allowed two catches on eight targets. His fourth-quarter interception at the 28-yard line secured three points (to extend to a 14-point lead) at a time when the offense couldn't move the ball.

"I mean, I'm glad that [Harbaugh] thinks that," Adebo said. "I certainly think highly of myself. So, kind of been thinking that way the whole season. So, it wasn't anything new per se."

The stretch run of the season suddenly becomes a proving ground for Adebo, who recently looked like a lock to be a salary cap casualty ($14 million saved against $6.3 million dead money) in 2027. Could the Giants feel comfortable with Adebo and Newsome as starters next year if Banks leaves in free agency?

"He's a baller," safety Jevón Holland said. "He hasn't been playing that much this year, but it's just how the game goes. When they called his name, he went out and balled. I'm excited for him."

People snickered when Newsome prematurely declared the Giants cornerback room the best in the NFL during the preseason.

It's become harder to argue with his assessment as time has passed. The performance against Trevor Lawrence was a definitive argument because Banks added an interception and Dru Phillips made three of the most important plays (all breakups) of the game.

"I think I said it in training camp about how we have three, four guys that are really starters in this league," Adebo said. "So, there really is that mindset that we all know we can compete. So, just keep on executing, keep on doing what we've been doing."

#2685. Wizards’ Brian Keefe sends strong message on failed comeback attempt vs. Celtics

Wizards' Brian Keefe sends strong message on failed comeback attempt vs. Celtics appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Washington Wizards have been playing much better basketball as of late. #2683. No, the Chicago Bears are Not in Salary Cap Hell

Yesterday, someone shared on social media (no, I won't elevate their account here) that the Chicago Bears had a dire salary cap situation looming in 2027. It's not the biggest account, and it's not even a sports-related account, but it picked up a bunch of traction as others shared it. Some believed the erroneous content and complained the Bears were screwed or wanted to lol at the stupid Bears, while others wanted to dunk on how wrong the take was.

A prominent gambling account (I won't elevate this account either) picked up on the narrative and, to gain clicks and engagement, shared their own version of the "oh no, the Bears are in cap Hell in 2027!" tweet to all their followers too.

For those not on social media, Bears fans are an easy mark. And that's not meant negatively; our fanbase is very, very active and passionate, and if you want to bait us or troll us, you're going to get a reaction.

ESPN's Field Yates, who knows a thing or two about the NFL and the salary cap, came across that above-referenced tweet and shared, "I don't know why this notion that the Bears are facing salary cap straits in 2027 has generated traction recently, but it's… not true. There are so many pathways for this team to easily clear cap room next year. And extend Caleb Williams."

Nick Korte, who is a contributor at Over the Cap and one of the best follows for salary cap and comp pick info, responded to Yates with this.

Simple restructures of the 5 players who the Bears have already devoted guaranteed salaries to in 2027 gets them in salary cap compliance without doing anything else. And the Bears have the 2nd highest availability in cap dollars in 2028 right now. Stay chill, Bears fans.

So even though Chicago's cap looks tight in 2027, and Caleb's extension will be a big one, there's nothing to worry about.

#2682. Jensen Huang's Nvidia return shows the U.S.-China AI race is about more than chips

BEIJING — Jensen Huang's return to Nvidia as its CEO underscores how the U.S.-China artificial intelligence race is not only about semiconductors, but tech talent.

"We have to protect our geniuses, and he's clearly a genius," Donald Trump told CNBC about his intervention to bring Huang back. If not, Trump warned, the founder of the AI chip giant could hypothetically have taken a job in China.

The concern reflects how Chinese AI companies have been narrowing the gap with their U.S. counterparts, not just in models, but now in engineering and research talent. China has surpassed the U.S. by share of global AI talent in just three years, according to Carnegie Endowment research.

Nvidia "was shaken without a single chip or model changing," said Winston Ma, an AI investor and adjunct professor of law at New York University. "Whoever builds and keeps that human layer will hold the rest of the stack."

Huang resigned Nov. 12 after reports of an affair with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes has links to Chinese intelligence. He returned on Nov. 23, news which sent Nvidia shares surging.

More than 30 years ago, Huang founded Nvidia as a gaming graphics chip company. The semiconductors gradually became used to mine cryptocurrencies, and now, training cutting-edge AI models.

Huang has more advanced tech coming, Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers on Monday.

"He's got other chips in his brain that nobody else knows about, including me, that nobody else understands," Trump said.

"Jensen could take the next plane to China, and I guarantee you, they would give him whatever he wanted, and then and then they automatically have, you know, all of the things," Trump said. He acknowledged there was "never even a suggestion" that the Nvidia founder was recruited by China.

Trump returned to the U.S. over the weekend from Shenzhen, China, where he met Chinese President Xi Jinping. Earlier in the month, turmoil around Huang's future at Nvidia had gripped global attention.

For Huang's supporters, talent was a critical part of the argument, said Damien Ma, director and Maurice R. Greenberg Director's Chair of Carnegie China.

As the incident has increased scrutiny on the people involved in the U.S.-China tech race, Ma said he's closely watching whether either country restricts AI engineers from leaving. While he expects limited policy changes, other countries such as Singapore and South Korea could benefit from an inflow of AI talent, he added.

Already, many companies are using a mixed-market approach, given engineers in the U.S. make two or three times as much as their Chinese counterparts due in part to foreign exchange rates and AI company valuations.

In the U.S., "you need to have a lot of money to recruit them," said Alex Lu, founder of LSY Consulting — and that can get expensive.

Lu cited the founder of a U.S. consumer research firm, who opted to hire engineers based in China after visiting the country.

It's an anecdote that's frequently repeated by analysts, sometimes with U.S.-trained Chinese staff based in Singapore.

Chinese companies are "very, very aggressive in getting good talent from the U.S.," said Tidalwave Solutions Partner Rachel Wu, who recruits for large Chinese tech companies and startups.

She also said a Meta engineer in Canada took an offer from a Chinese AI startup for a salary of 1.4 million yuan ($210,000), in addition to stock options.

"There is some truth to Trump's comments given Jensen's knowledge of the AI industry and supply chain," said Lian Jye Su, a chief analyst of Omdia. "It would be unwise for the U.S. to let this saga continue to affect its AI ambition, especially when that ambition includes slowing down Chinese progress in AI."

However, Rui Ma, founder of advisory Tech Buzz China, said Huang's background of being a founder makes it more likely that he would have started his own company, rather than taking a managerial role at one.

She also noted Huang's "deep belief in America" and laws that would block him from using his access to TSMC and other suppliers to help a Chinese company.

The U.S. has restricted China's access to advanced Nvidia semiconductors over the last four years. But in early 2025, DeepSeek upended assumptions that chip restrictions could keep Chinese AI capabilities well behind that of the U.S.

A few years ago, only the U.S. and Singapore had the kind of AI talent that Chinese companies were looking for, Wu said. "Today we see the gap has been filled," she said, adding the supply of local talent has increased in the last two to three years.

"The demand is huge," Wu said, saying one ex-Alibaba AI engineering candidate with seven years of experience had more than five offers.

Switching between companies can result in a 25% to 30% pay bump, Wu said. She expects the trend to persist for another two years, although the increase in compensation may not be as high.

Still, restricted access to advanced AI chips plays a role in where the best researchers choose to work.

It's a question of "where I can do my further research and do I have enough resources to do my research," said Lu.

He said industry research suggests "China is becoming a really important pool for talent, while the U.S. is paying 80% of the money."

The Utah Utes sit alone atop college football as the only unbeaten team in the FBS, and quarterback Devon Dampier is soaking in every second of the ride.
