#2680. Trump wanted Jensen Huang back. Now, the president is all alone in Washington

President Donald Trump wanted Jensen Huang back at the helm of Nvidia. Now that the chip mogul is reinstated, Trump is cheering alone in Washington.

Trump celebrated and claimed credit after Huang was reinstated by the Nvidia board on Monday, a stunning return to power for the embattled CEO. In a nearly hourlong phone call with CNBC, the president went as far as saying he "made the deal" to bring Huang back.

But Trump's jubilation stands in stark contrast to the reaction from even his staunchest allies on Capitol Hill, who recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang — whose relationship with a potential Chinese intelligence asset they fear may have jeopardized U.S. national security.

"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States."

Trump's rare isolation from his party over Huang comes after the GOP was throttled in the midterm elections, as Democrats rode a blue tsunami to capture the Senate and the largest House majority this century. And it suggests Republicans, who have spent a decade in lockstep with the president, may have found an issue on which they can't bring themselves to take Trump's side.

"I can accept what the president has done," Cramer said. "But we have to put guardrails around to protect the United States of America."

Many midterm voters identified Silicon Valley's most visible outputs, artificial intelligence and data centers, as top concerns motivating them to cast ballots for Democrats. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey in October found 63% of registered voters oppose an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% support one.

By timing his extraordinary intervention in Nvidia so soon after the GOP's historic losses, Trump could spark a national debate about corporate governance and responsibility, former Under Secretary of State Richard Stengel said.

"With the repudiation of Trumpism in the midterms and voter revulsion with corruption, I think there will be a turn back to a more value-based duty of care for boards of directors and away from the current maximization of shareholder value," Stengel told CNBC in an email.

"Directors are citizens first, shareholders second. The idea that you put profit above national security should disgust shareholders, not please them," Stengel, who worked in the Obama administration, wrote.

Bipartisan lawmakers are now racing to pass a series of export control bills before the year ends, a call that has only grown louder since Huang was reinstated.

There's "not just frustration or political worry, but actual, substantive national security concern here from Democrats and Republicans," Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, told CNBC.

"This has historically been a bipartisan issue," McGuire said. "Democrats and Republicans generally pretty much agree that we shouldn't be sending our most advanced chips to China."

"If it happened in massive quantities, people want to know why, how, and they want to make sure that it doesn't happen again," he said. "And putting your thumb on the scale in this clear of a way seems like it could undercut those efforts."

The White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on the political dynamics of Trump's involvement in reinstating Huang.

Republicans will have the first crack at investigating Huang next week before ceding power to Democrats in January. Democrats have vowed their own investigations into the Nvidia CEO and his ties to the president and his Cabinet. The Democrats who will lead those investigations are now hopeful that Republicans will join them.

"I've been seeing a lot of Republicans outraged and scandalized by what has just happened," House Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin, D-Md., said Tuesday on "Squawk Box," when asked if Republicans may support a bipartisan investigation of Nvidia.

"When else in American history can you think of a time when a president of the United States called up a corporate board of directors meeting and then essentially dictated to them who was going to be their new CEO right after they had fired the guy?"

Trump throughout his second term has stated repeatedly that achieving AI dominance is required for the U.S. to maintain global superiority. His administration has championed the rapid growth of the country's tech giants and forged close ties with its leaders, especially Huang, while removing constraints on the industry and opposing some new regulatory efforts.

At the same time, Trump has defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick against accusations of slow-walking export controls on China, the fiercest competitor in the AI race. And while Trump recently discussed AI during an in-person meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, he appeared to deliver few demands and achieved few assurances about Beijing's efforts to acquire U.S. chips.

"To say we can't do AI safety because of China, and then also be actively loosening the controls on China, makes no sense," McGuire said.

Some of Trump's fiercest allies have raised similar qualms.

"Reinstating Jensen Huang is going to blow back on our national security," MAGA activist Laura Loomer wrote in an X post Monday. "Eventually there has to be accountability for jeopardizing US national security. China didn't just get access to Nvidia chips by accident."

Newt Gingrich, the influential former Republican speaker of the House, in an X post after Huang's reinstatement, wrote, "Congress should really dig into this as a national security issue of enormous proportions and also a significant failure in the federal bureaucracy."

Their criticisms sharply contrast with the praise coming from Silicon Valley and some of the Trump-friendly voices within it, such as Elon Musk, who wrote Monday, "The mistake has been corrected."

But that's a shrinking political constituency, McGuire said.

"The tech-right perspective is very out of touch with where most of the Republican Party is on these issues, to say nothing of the Democratic Party," he said.

"Those positions are just not going to be sustainable for much longer," he added. "I think they're already becoming unsustainable."

#2679. Trump Account contributions from friends and family pass $1 billion mark, Treasury says

Families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts since the July 4 launch, bringing total deposits to more than $9 billion, the U.S. Department of the Treasury said Tuesday in an announcement exclusively provided to CNBC.

The $1 billion in Trump Account deposits come from more than 3 million individuals, according to the agency. That total does not include the government's seed money or philanthropic gifts from donors such as Michael and Susan Dell.

"The American people are investing in Trump Accounts with a generosity that represents the finest instincts of our national character," U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in a written statement.

"Trump Accounts are a transformative government program for American children. It is the largest government start-up in American history," Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano said in the statement.

Also known as 530A accounts, Trump Accounts are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

Most recently, Snowflake chairman Frank Slootman and his wife, Brenda, announced they would give $1,000 to every child in Montana born in 2023 and 2024.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move meant to pave the way for more philanthropic gifts, according to Bisignano.

These rules make it more attractive for donors, according to Ben Henry-Moreland, a certified financial planner with advisor platform Kitces.com.

By gifting stocks, wealthy founders and shareholders can avoid the capital gains taxes that would otherwise be triggered by selling appreciated stock and donating the cash proceeds. However, it also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

The Trump Accounts milestone comes as more billionaires and companies pledge support to the investment account for kids.

In an exclusive interview with CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," Bessent on Nov. 20 announced an Anthropic co-founder would make a "large gift" to Trump Accounts over the next few weeks.

A person briefed on the announcement confirmed that the Anthropic co-founder Bessent referred to is Tom Brown and that it is part of his broader philanthropic plan. The person asked not to be named because the matter is private.

"This is the perfect vehicle to donate to the children of America, and they will receive 100% of the proceeds," Bessent said.

Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which experts say may come via Trump Accounts.

In a separate funding announcement, SpaceX President Gwynne Shotwell said she would donate around 2 million shares of her and her husband's SpaceX stock to the accounts.

On Monday, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner, founder of Invest America and an architect of Trump Accounts, told CNBC's "Halftime Report" that more large-scale donations may be in the pipeline.

"I want everybody to own shares of Nvidia, in Anthropic, in OpenAI," he said. "We created 70 million stock accounts — free stock accounts — for every single citizen under the age of 18 to do just that, and we have huge announcements coming."

#2676. Jensen Huang to face DC grilling the same day Nvidia releases new products at GTC

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's artificial intelligence vision while also defending himself in Washington.

As Nvidia employees celebrated the return of their leader to the company's headquarters, Huang told the gathered crowd, "I'll be a better CEO the second time around."

Huang will need to be a better CEO to head off the issues surrounding Nvidia and the broader AI industry exposed by Huang's exit. The challenges include a proliferating roster of investigations and hearings from Congress, regulators and state-level officials, as well as technical and competitive questions about how long Nvidia's currently dominant graphics processing units can remain on top.

"There's nothing prohibiting him from doing what he does on his day job," said Katten chair and former federal prosecutor Gil Soffer. But investigations take significant prep time, Soffer said. "You don't just walk in cold."

At Nvidia's GTC conference in Washington, D.C., next week, the company will attempt to convince customers, partners and suppliers that it is developing the future of AI. At the same time, lawmakers will be pressing its newly reinstated CEO over what he knew and when about Megaspeed executive Alice Huang.

Huang, before his scandals, was scheduled to give the GTC DC keynote speech on Dec. 1 to discuss new Nvidia products and the trajectory of the AI ecosystem. In the past, the two-hour speech was the highlight of the event with hordes of fans full of the Huang-inspired "Jensanity."

"People will want to see that it's business as usual, which hopefully for Nvidia shareholders will mean that Jensen Huang announces some new exciting products," said Babson College associate professor Peter Cohan.

Right now, Nvidia has Ian Buck, vice president of hyperscale and high-performance computing, filling the slot. A representative for Nvidia declined to answer multiple questions about whether Huang would appear at the company's event.

On the same day, Huang is scheduled to speak with three House committees — Foreign Affairs, Select Committee on the CCP, and the Select Committee on Intelligence — behind closed doors, where his interview will be transcribed.

"The NVIDIA board's brief internal investigation and reinstatement of Jensen Huang do not erase the national security threats and questions that must be addressed," the chairs said in a joint statement. They said that Huang has confirmed he will appear for the transcribed interview on Dec. 1.

Next Tuesday's interviews are just the first of a slew of investigations.

On Dec. 3, just as Nvidia's conference is wrapping up, the Senate Banking Committee will hold a hearing titled "Protecting America's AI Advantage: Oversight of U.S. Export Controls." Sen. Tim Scott, R-S.C., who chairs the committee, said Huang will publicly testify as the sole witness.

California's attorney general is also investigating Huang's remarks, and politicians from the Senate Finance, Intelligence, and Foreign Relations committees have inquired about the scandal. In the House, Energy and Commerce has requested a company briefing from Nvidia, in addition to the three committees mentioned earlier. That's not counting an investigation from the Department of Justice and a reported investigation from the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Plus, Huang still has to cooperate with an investigation from the board of directors, led by the law firm Wachtell Lipton.

Meanwhile, Nvidia needs Huang to reassure investors and the broader AI ecosystem that the company is developing new products that will be in high demand. Investors had said that while Nvidia's products for the next two years have been fully designed, they were worried that the chipmaker's pace of innovation would slow without Huang.

Nvidia is facing new competition from chips from its biggest customers, including OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Amazon and Microsoft. The companies have been developing their own chips to reduce reliance on Nvidia, as well as to control AI costs.

"There was already this worry that we're spending too much on it," said Stephanie Balaouras, vice president of group research at Forrester. "One of those cost management strategies is to match ... the workload requirements to the right infrastructure. You don't need to put everything on a Nvidia GPU."

Many in the AI industry see Huang as one of the staunchest defenders of "open-source" AI that is free to use for commercial purposes, as compared with "frontier models" from companies like Anthropic and OpenAI, which charge users by the token.

Nvidia has also been spending heavily to develop its own open-source models called Nemotron. Currently, Nvidia Vice President Kari Briski is scheduled to present new Nemotron technology at GTC.

On Monday, Nvidia's board said that "senior levels" of the U.S. government provided input on Huang's potential national security risk. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he was central to convincing Nvidia's board to reinstate Huang and said he gave the board assurances.

Trump's help could draw even more scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers, who were already focused on probing Nvidia's chip sales.

"Today's news that Donald Trump engineered the rehiring of Jensen Huang at Nvidia is Exhibit A about the political corruption and rot at the heart of the Trump administration's big tech and AI policy," said Democratic Sen. Chris Van Hollen of Maryland in a call with reporters Monday.

Republicans have also criticized Huang's return as the focus on the company's China sales intensifies. Still, the investigations could start to weigh on Nvidia's CEO, experts say.

"How could he possibly do the job?" Jeff Sonnenfeld, senior associate dean at the Yale School of Management, told CNBC, citing Senate investigations, House investigations and other state investigations, even before Democrats take over next year.

And the reaction in Washington to Huang's scandal could start to affect Nvidia's sales.

Last week, the Department of Commerce announced that it will prevent Chinese companies from getting around export controls by renting Nvidia's chips across borders. On Monday, Commerce also added Megaspeed to a trade blacklist.

And it's not just Nvidia that is relying on a strong performance from Huang. The company's investors, customers, partners and shareholders will also look to see if Nvidia still wants to be at the center of the AI ecosystem. Before Huang was removed, he pushed Nvidia to invest in AI startups, while selling them products, and helping finance their infrastructure.

Remarks from Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress last week while she was interim CEO suggested that Nvidia would be taking a "very strong look" at using its balance sheet to support the AI ecosystem, but it's unclear if Huang's second stint as CEO will continue with his prior showmanship or abide by a more measured approach.

#2675. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2674. Knicks’ lack of wing depth is rearing ugly head with recent injuries

Since the mountain of injuries single-handedly derailed the Knicks' 2024 playoff run, they've been the pinnacle of health around the NBA.

Aside from dealing with the lack of durability from Mitchell Robinson, almost no long-term injuries have hampered the Knicks' playoff aspirations. Even the more bothersome injuries, Landry Shamet's shoulder issues, Deuce McBride's core surgery, and Jalen Brunson's nagging ankles, all resolve themselves before the postseason.

Coming off a grueling championship run and attempting an exceptionally difficult repeat in an age of parity, injuries are the looming threat that could derail things before they get started.

It's no wonder, then, that the team has been playing it safe with minor ailments to Jose Alvarado and Josh Hart over the last few weeks, but two more issues have sprung up over the last few days. Jordan Clarkson is dealing with a bout of plantar fasciitis, and OG Anunoby has a calf strain.

Both of these could easily be resolved in under a week, but could both also derail both of their seasons.

Plantar fasciitis can be managed, which is likely what the team is trying to do, but more severe bouts that build up over time could leave players out for months. Calf injuries in recent years have been a dangerous precursor for Achilles tears, as seen with Kevin Durant and Tyrese Haliburton in recent years. Even if it's a mild strain, it is imperative to take it slow.

That might mean no Anunoby for a while, even if no timetable has been presented. For a coach like Mike Brown who loves to experiment in the regular season, that might not seem like too bad.

For us fans? It means seeing some… interesting lineup combinations in close games. It's one thing when they played around with Dalton Knecht and Mo Diawara against the Bucks and Bulls, but Pacôme Dadiet? Now that's something.

Landry Shamet seems to have replaced Anunoby in the starting lineup, and although his incredible shooting and great defense are providing a spark to a starting five that usually starts sluggish (Starters w/Shamet replacing Anunoby have a +29.4 net rating in 15 minutes, compared to -12.9 with the regulars), he lacks size.

This presents a problem that the Knicks knew they had, but it has further exacerbated the team's quietest shortcoming: wing depth.

In a perfect world, Diawara would've taken the next step in his development and firmly entrenched himself in the rotation, but he's been passed over for Dadiet in the last week for those bench wing minutes vacated by the injuries. Part of me thinks it's an attempt to raise the expiring French wing's trade value, but it could be something in practice prompting this opportunity.

Since Diawara signed his four-year extension in July, there's been no sign of improvement. He struggled in Summer League and has been fairly regular in any action he's had since. That's opened the door for Dadiet, who, although seems more confident from three-point range, has also been unspectacular.

It's hard to hide them in a lineup offensively given the roster construction. Sure, ideally, you have Karl-Anthony Towns or Jalen Brunson on the court at all times, but that's just one player. When one is off the court, either Tyler Kolek or Andre Drummond is on, and neither are any threat to score.

Surround that with Hart's offensive struggles, Mikal Bridges' inconsistency, and the limited games of McBride and Shamet, and you have some painful offensive stretches. When Brown gets more involved, it gets better, but teams don't want to be constantly running stuff:

The options are extremely limited, and if you try to look deeper than Diawara or Dadiet, you get two guys who shoot and don't do much else (Tyler Nickel, Knecht). That's it.

This isn't that big of a deal if Anunoby's strain is mild and he's back soon. Injuries happen, and everyone has to eventually play the end of the bench in unenviable situations over a grueling 82-game season, so Brown will just hold his nose and rely on his army of small-to-medium-sized guards in these spots and hope they won't get burned when the better teams eventually come.

But long term, could the Knicks use another warm body?

The Knicks are unable to sign a 15th player until February 13, squarely in buyout market territory, and it's far too soon to tell who could be available. There's also the trade deadline with some interesting contract situations on the roster, but is getting an insurance policy the top priority over, say, an upgrade at backup center?

We're well past the Vince Goodwill fake panic of the first few weeks, and the Knicks are sitting pretty in a three-way tie at second in the East through 17 games, but maybe the front office will need to get to work on more than they originally thought they would have to.

#2673. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2672. Trump open to pardoning Jensen Huang if needed: NY Post

President Donald Trump said he would be willing to pardon reinstated Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang if needed after Huang appears before Congress next week, according to an exclusive interview with the New York Post.

Huang's reinstatement followed Trump's intervention with Nvidia's board, including a reported call into a board meeting to argue for his return. Even so, lawmakers are still set to scrutinize his relationship with suspected Chinese intelligence asset Alice Huang in two appearances next week.

Nvidia shares surged 31% on Monday after Huang was reinstated as CEO, before falling 5.9% on Tuesday amid a broader market pullback.

Trump also said that Huang should not need to invoke the Fifth Amendment to avoid self-incrimination when he appears for a closed-door interview with the House Foreign Affairs Committee on Dec. 1 and before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3.

"I think it's an interesting story for people," Trump said, according to the NY Post. "I think I want him to just tell the truth."

Trump added that while a pardon was "always an option," he did not think it would be "necessary," the newspaper reported. The president's clemency authority is limited to federal offenses, according to the Justice Department's Office of the Pardon Attorney.

Sen. John Kennedy, R-La., told Fox News on Monday that he expected Huang to invoke his Fifth Amendment rights during the congressional proceedings.

"I expect him to take the Fifth Amendment so he doesn't incriminate himself in criminal prosecution, but we're going to give him a chance to explain," Kennedy said.

Trump also told the New York Post that he was open to the U.S. government taking an equity stake in Nvidia, similar to its stake in Intel in 2025, arguing that the company's success was vital to maintaining the U.S. lead in artificial intelligence.

He also said it was his understanding that Huang had not had affairs with other women linked to Beijing and that he did not know how many affairs Huang had acknowledged.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., the lead Democrat on the House Judiciary Committee, told CNBC on Tuesday that he believed Trump was making decisions that could benefit his own stock portfolio, despite the president's assertion that he does not make trades himself.

"If you don't believe that, you're too innocent to be let out of the house by yourself. The whole thing is a money-making operation. What's the alternative explanation?" Raskin said.

Separately, independent journalist Marisa Kabas reported that Huang told Nvidia employees at Tuesday's all-hands meeting that Trump "loves" the company because of its employees' "contributions" to the U.S., while also addressing questions about whether Nvidia had become political or partisan.

"I want to be very clear: President Trump is the United States president. I'll do everything I can to help this president be informed about how he can make decisions in the best interests of the country," Huang said.

The reinstated CEO added that it would be a "great honor and a privilege" to go to Washington, D.C., to "inform them so the government can make the best possible decision," and said he would do the same for whoever becomes the next president.

"Don't think for a second politics played a role in the company. We are the most apolitical company. Everything he's done is because of his affection, admiration, and recognition of the great company that you are," Huang said.

#2671. U.S. jury convicts Huawei in a criminal trial over fraud and trade secrets

Chinese technology giant Huawei was convicted Tuesday in New York on multiple federal criminal charges, including fraud, U.S. sanctions violations and conspiracy to steal trade secrets, following a three-month trial, according to the U.S. Department of Justice.

The verdict marks a major development in a long-running criminal case against Huawei, with charges first publicly disclosed in 2019.

A federal jury found Huawei and three of its subsidiaries guilty of multiple offenses, including bank and wire fraud and conspiracy to commit money laundering, the Justice Department said in a statement.

"No person or company is above the law," U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche said, accusing Huawei and its subsidiaries of conducting business in the United States through deception and theft.

The Justice Department said Huawei misrepresented its activities in Iran to banks to secure billions of dollars and retain access to U.S. dollar-clearing services. The company also supplied surveillance cameras that enabled Iranian authorities to monitor protests, the department said.

Among the examples of intellectual property theft detailed by prosecutors were cases involving Cisco and T-Mobile.

Huawei copied source code used in Cisco routers, with its engineers referring to Cisco by spelling its name backward in an attempt to conceal the theft.

Huawei employees also sent photographs and technical details of T-Mobile's touchscreen-testing robot to China, with one employee later removing a component from T-Mobile's laboratory in a backpack, according to the Justice Department.

"For over two decades, Huawei built its global empire on a foundation of fraud, theft, and deception," said Assistant Attorney General Tysen A. Duva of the Justice Department's Criminal Division.

Huawei pushed back against the verdict in a statement issued Wednesday, while welcoming its acquittal on the racketeering conspiracy charge.

"We believe the government's remaining charges are equally without legal or factual merit," Huawei said, adding that the five trade secret-related disputes cited by prosecutors had previously been resolved as civil matters.

The company said it would review the verdicts and take necessary legal action to defend its interests, while remaining focused on maintaining uninterrupted business operations and investing in research and development.

Huawei and its subsidiaries face potential financial penalties, including fines, forfeiture and restitution, with sentencing scheduled for Sept. 14, 2027, according to the Justice Department.

U.S. prosecutors first publicly disclosed criminal charges against Huawei in January 2019, a month after the company's chief financial officer, Meng Wanzhou, was arrested in Canada.

Meng returned to China in September 2021 after entering into a deferred prosecution agreement with U.S. prosecutors.

#2670. “Only one club in Manchester”: former Man United star Rasmus Hojlund taunts rivals after Champions League strike

Manchester United have had the most difficult start to a season in memory.

One of their biggest issues has been in attack as first-choice striker Benjamin Šeško has spent most of the season on the sidelines with injury.

This has meant Matheus Cunha and Bryan Mbeumo have often had to play out of position, naturally affecting their form and the team's fluidity.

Joshua Zirkzee has also rarely been given much of an opportunity to get involved and, when he has, he has hardly set the world on fire.

#2669. The Bellingham effect, Vinicius’ lifeline | Three takeaways from PSV 1-5 Real Madrid

A disastrous first-half performance from Real Madrid and an early penalty for PSV handed the visitors the lead at the Santiago Bernabeu last night.

They held the lead heading into the half-time break, but what followed after they returned to the field was nothing less than a hurricane.

Real Madrid came back after the break more motivated than ever and began suffocating PSV in their own area with brilliant attacking football. A great goal from Federico Valverde then handed them an equaliser.

Jude Bellingham, Kylian Mbappe and Vinicius Jr. then joined the party and added four goals to the scoreline, completing PSV's demolition and registering a massive scoreline.

Madrid Universal brings you three takeaways from Real Madrid 5-1 PSV Eindhoven.

Jose Mourinho's starting XI against PSV Eindhoven saw the manager make some big changes, and understandably so, given how crucial rotations are.

Jude Bellingham scored a sensational goal vs PSV. (Photo by Angel Martinez/Getty Images)

Dean Huijsen, Ibrahima Konate and Jude Bellingham all started on the bench, and all three came on later in the game to reprieve the team.

Of them, Bellingham's cameo was the one that well and truly turned the game around, and in some style.

With the Englishman on the bench, Real Madrid struggled to dominate the game or create chances of their own. They created an xG of just 0.24 compared to PSV's 1.50, and conceded four big chances.

Throughout the first half, Los Blancos looked unmotivated, sluggish and slow to react to PSV's attacks, but everything changed after Bellingham came on.

The Englishman instantly brought purpose, energy and directness to Real Madrid's play, catalysing the team's performance and bringing the best version out of his teammates.

In 45 minutes, he racked up three key passes, earned one assist and scored one goal against PSV while also completing 95% of his passes. His presence on the field brought verticality to Real Madrid's buildup, and he alone provided eight carries and completed one dribble.

It is no surprise that the second-half xG for Real Madrid went up to a staggering 4.53 – a clear indication of how ruthless the team became with the Englishman on the field.

As promised, Jose Mourinho handed Vinicius Jr. a start against PSV in the UEFA Champions League last night, and the player was under serious pressure to perform as he was staring at a real risk of losing his starting spot in the team.

Vinicius Jr. finally found the net for Real Madrid. (Photo by Angel Martinez/Getty Images)

His performance last night may not have been vintage, but it surely was a big step up from where he has been all season.

Vinicius played all 90 minutes at the Santiago Bernabeu, and he finally looked like a genuine goalscoring threat. He was lively down the left flank, and while he may not have shown his dribbling best, he did show glimpses of his vintage self.

In 90 minutes, the winger took four shots and scored one goal for Real Madrid. He hit the woodwork twice and missed two chances.

Further, the player looked connected to the buildup as he completed 83% of his passes and even earned one key pass. Vinicius completed one successful dribble and made a staggering ten progressive carries.

There is indeed still a long way to go before Vinicius returns to his Ballon d'Or campaign level, but Real Madrid and Mourinho will be encouraged by what they saw last night. Between his great international break and decent start to club football after the hiatus, there is reason to believe the old Vinicius could return.

The win over the Dutch opposition last night was Real Madrid's fifth in as many UEFA Champions League games this season, and takes them right to the top of the table, and PSG remain the only other side that can catch up in terms of points.

Real Madrid are unbeaten in the Champions League. (Photo by Angel Martinez/Getty Images)

Just five matchdays into the campaign, Los Blancos already have fifteen points to their name and are now within touching distance of a top-8 finish.

Over the past two seasons, Real Madrid have consistently missed out on the top-8 by narrow margins, and have been forced to play an additional round in the competition in both seasons.

Historically, 16 points have been enough to secure a top-8 finish, and with Real Madrid already having fifteen with three games to go, it is safe to say they have all but qualified.

Just one point in their next three games will be enough to secure a top-8 finish, and while Arsenal may be a tricky opposition, they have games against LASK and Shakhtar remaining.

The target for Jose Mourinho and Co., needless to say, will be to finish as high as possible on the standings and thereby earn the second-leg home advantage all the way through the knockouts. Given their current position, even a top-1 finish is completely possible.

#2667. SoftBank drops 15% as Wall Street AI sell-off ripples across Asian tech names

Asian technology stocks tumbled Wednesday, led by a rout in Japanese semiconductor stocks, as renewed concerns over the artificial intelligence trade rattles investors following an overnight sell-off on Wall Street.

Japanese semiconductor stocks tied to the global AI supply chain were among the hardest hit. Advantest, a supplier of chip-testing equipment to Nvidia, plunged 13%. Tokyo Electron, which supplies chipmaking equipment to Nvidia's manufacturing partner TSMC, slumped 12.82%.

Technology-focused investor SoftBank Group shed 15%, while semiconductor inspection equipment maker Lasertec lost 11.23%.

Other Japanese chip stocks also came under heavy selling pressure, with Disco Corporation falling 9.41% and Renesas Electronics losing 9%.

Tech stocks have seen heightened volatility over the past few months amid concerns over capital expenditure, skepticism over return on investments, circular AI financing, with Jensen Huang's ouster as Nvidia CEO adding fresh worries, and his return sparking a massive rally.

Rolf Bulk, an analyst at The Futurum Group, said the renewed sell-off largely reflects lingering concerns over the sustainability of AI spending beyond 2027, with Huang's return doing little to fundamentally alter the outlook.

"There are still concerns on the sustainability of AI demand, ability of the sector to finance the build-out, and questions around power supply," Bulk said, adding that Japanese chip equipment makers were particularly vulnerable to a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure spending, given the highly cyclical nature of their business.

Spending on wafer fabrication equipment could decline materially if AI demand slows from 2028 onward, Bulk said. South Korean memory chipmakers, by contrast, could be relatively insulated due to supply shortages.

The country's memory chip giants Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix, however, plunged more than 8%. The two are key suppliers of the high-bandwidth memory chips used in AI accelerators. Other South Korean technology stocks also traded lower, with LG Innotek shedding 5%.

While the memory industry is also cyclical, Bulk expects shortages to intensify over the next two years, making a return to equilibrium between supply and demand in 2028 unlikely.

In Taiwan, shares TSMC tumbled 7%, while Hon Hai Precision Industry plunged 8.6%. Electronic components maker Foxconn Technology shed 4.93%.

Some Chinese technology stocks listed in Hong Kong bucked the broader regional sell-off, with Xiaomi jumping 4.6%, Kuaishou gaining 2.8% and Alibaba adding 0.7%. Tencent was marginally higher, while Meituan and Baidu was up 0.31% and 0.16% respectively.

U.S. technology stocks fell overnight with the Nasdaq Composite giving back some of the sharp gains from the previous session that were fueled by Huang's return as Nvidia CEO.

#2666. Crypto talent will return ‘when AI implodes,’ Arthur Hayes says

Startup talent that has concentrated in artificial intelligence will return to the crypto industry "when AI implodes," according to Arthur Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said in an online interview with CNBC on Tuesday.

He added that some founders may also return once they "recognize" that their products could eventually be "usurped by Anthropic or OpenAI."

The scale and capabilities of the largest frontier AI labs have made it difficult to "build a real AI business," Hayes said, arguing that "most" founders simply "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to face "serious headwinds" in the second half of 2027, when leading labs begin confronting sizable bills tied to compute leases and other non-cancelable commitments.

Anthropic expects about $451.6 billion in compute and infrastructure obligations through the end of 2030, with roughly $70.7 billion due by the end of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Broader enthusiasm around the AI trade has also cooled amid questions about future infrastructure spending. Even exuberance from Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO on Monday proved short-lived, with the shares falling 5.9% on Tuesday and Anthropic dropping below its IPO price four days after its debut.

The co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX also thinks bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to fall. The cryptocurrency has shown little sign of recovery after dropping as low as $55,142.16 on Saturday — its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin hit an all-time high of $126,279.63 in October last year and traded as high as $97,915.90 in January this year.

It has now fallen for seven straight weeks — only the third such streak in its history — with crypto exchange Bitfinex attributing the weakness to equity-market sentiment rather than interest-rate expectations.

Hayes said he would like to see bitcoin rebound from around $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to fall. That divergence, he said, would suggest markets were shifting from a broad sell-off toward pricing in a "reflation" scenario, with the Federal Reserve eventually providing more liquidity.

Neither bitcoin nor gold has yet decoupled from the Nasdaq, with Hayes describing the current market moves as a "correlation-led liquidation."

"Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said.

Hayes said a bitcoin rally could push up funding rates and futures-basis returns, increasing the income Ethena earns from its hedged crypto positions. That could make USDe, Ethena's synthetic dollar, more attractive and expand its circulation, he said, adding that he expects the ENA token to rise materially.

"Those are the two things that I'm keeping an eye on, and when I'm ready to pull the trigger on buying risk again, those will be the things we buy a lot of," Hayes said.

#2665. Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers

Observations after Sixers' 5-game win streak ends with blowout loss to Blazers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers' five-game winning streak was snapped Monday night by the Trail Blazers.

With a 128-111 defeat at Xfinity Mobile Arena, the Sixers dropped their first game of the year against Western Conference opposition and fell to 14-4. Portland improved to 11-6.

LeBron James had a season-high 23 points and six rebounds. Tyrese Maxey scored 22 points and Jaylen Brown added 20.

Deni Avdija led the Blazers with 31 points and seven assists. Donovan Clingan posted 15 points and 18 rebounds.

The Sixers were missing Joel Embiid (illness). Ja Morant (left hamstring strain) and Shaedon Sharpe (right knee meniscus surgery) were among Portland's sidelined players.

Next on the Sixers' schedule is a Wednesday night game vs. the Heat. Here are observations on their blowout loss to the Blazers:

Jabari Walker, listed at 6-foot-7, defied conventional wisdom by winning the opening tip-off against the 7-2 Cllingan. Maxey then canned a three-pointer.

Brown scored the Sixers' next four points with a pair of free throws and a short jumper. VJ Edgecombe's layup gave the team a 9-2 lead. Brown and Dean Wade both returned Monday after sitting out the last two games with left hamstring injuries.

Avdija got hot in the first quarter. He scored 11 quick points, made his first four field goals and helped the Blazers avoid a serious early deficit.

The Sixers took a 32-30 edge with 9.3 seconds left in the first when Walker jammed in a put-back dunk. The former Portland second-round draft pick played 17 minutes in his second start of the season and continued to look deserving of a rotation spot. He posted 10 points and five rebounds.

Adem Bona was available again following an eight-game absence with a cervical spine sprain. However, Dominick Barlow checked in first to back up Walker.

Barlow had an outstanding start to his night. He scored a clever lefty push shot over Clingan and threw down a dunk in the first quarter. Barlow also made a highlight defensive play in his opening stint, defending Avdija in isolation and rejecting his layup attempt.

Bona then subbed in for Barlow with 3:23 left in the first quarter. He had a positive first possession, grabbing an offensive rebound and driving in for a dunk.

Wade later played at center, too. Barlow logged 16 minutes and had six points. Bona recorded four points and two boards in five minutes. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse ended up using a 10-man rotation and did not play rookie guard Labaron Philon Jr. until fourth-quarter garbage time.

Overall, the Sixers' defense remained considerably worse without Embiid. The team also conceded 15 offensive rebounds, including five to Clingan.

"I think your rim protection and ability to do that with some size is always a focal point of being really good defensively," Nurse said. "And then you've got to do it a little bit by committee (with Embiid out). I thought, for the most part, I was looking at possessions where I liked our help into the paint. They were doing a good job of zinging it out.

"I didn't particularly like our (defense) back out. … There were a couple of times they missed those and still got rebounds. We were compacted in the lane with everybody because we didn't get back out, and then still giving up an offensive rebound as well. That's just playing through the possession, a little bit of energy, all that kind of stuff. … We just didn't do enough pieces well."

Brown delivered tons of efficient offense for the Sixers in the first half and sure didn't appear rusty. His layup late in the second quarter tied the game at 58-all. The five-time All-Star scored 19 first-half points on 8-for-10 shooting.

Avdija exceeded Brown's production, hitting heat-check threes, leaners through contact and just about everything he put up regardless of the defender. He tallied 25 points in the first half and shot 9 for 12 from the field.

James reached 16 points when he capped a high-scoring half by nailing a corner three. The contest was even at 69 apiece heading into halftime.

Monday's game was James' first of the season with three or more made three-pointers. The four-time MVP went 4 for 5 beyond the arc. He'd gone 10 for 46 (21.7 percent) over his first 13 games and was due for a good long-range night.

James was whistled for his third foul early in the third quarter. Brown picked up his fourth with 10:15 to go in the period, forcing him to sit for an extended stretch.

"I wasn't too happy about it. … I think that affected the game," Brown said. "It also affected my rhythm a little bit in the second half. I had to sit (almost) the whole third quarter. Coming back from injury, coming back from a hamstring thing, my body didn't really feel the same after that."

Brown noted postgame that he had "a little bit of soreness and stuff like that."

"I think everything will check out," he said. "The medical staff is on top of everything. Those hamstrings are tricky, so you want to make sure you play it the right way, be safe. I also don't want to miss a lot of games, so I might be out there, but I've also got to be smart. I think I played it the right way today."

Avdija stopped lighting up the Sixers in the third period, but the team still had another unimpressive defensive quarter. Portland posted 32 points in the third and built its lead to double figures in the opening minute of the fourth.

The game devolved into a blowout. An Avdija dunk put the Blazers' advantage at 22 points with 5:15 on the clock and the Sixers didn't have a miracle comeback in store.

Even with all their wins, there's no question the Sixers have been trending downward on defense over the past couple of weeks, allowing regular dribble penetration and plenty of comfortable threes.

They'll aim to raise their level on that side of the floor and expect Embiid's presence to be a major help once he's back in action.

"I think it's just mindset," Walker said. "We're capable when we want to. You need games like this every now and then just to remind you that it's not as easy as it feels sometimes. It takes effort every game.

"I think we can learn a lot from this. It's just part of the growing pains, but I'm confident we'll get back to that (early-season) level of defense. We have what it takes."

#2664. ‘Ready for any storm’: UAE minister optimistic on economy as IMF lifts forecast

The UAE is "very optimistic" about achieving 7%-8% growth next year, the country's economy and tourism minister Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri told CNBC after the latest data from the International Monetary Fund showed the country would avoid a recession in 2026.

The IMF last week revised the UAE's real GDP forecast to 0.2% growth in 2026 from a 3.9% contraction predicted a month ago. "The UAE is coming back, and that adjustment within one month shows that there is a huge potential aspect when it comes towards the end of the year," the minister told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

He said the tourism industry was already picking up with occupancy rates returning to "almost near normal" and international carriers including British Airways and Air France rerouting flights to the UAE. The minister expects economic growth to be higher than 0.2% this year.

The Iran war has disrupted economic activity in the UAE, with missile and drone attacks from Tehran and maritime disruptions weighing on energy trade, tourism, aviation and private-sector confidence.

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump said that renewed attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" if negotiations produce a "very strong agreement." However, on Monday, Trump threatened that Tehran could face fresh U.S. strikes next year if the two countries failed to strike a deal, as per Fox News.

"We can we cannot promise the world for any storms that will land on our shore, but we can always promise that the UAE's economy is ready for any storm," Al Marri said.

He added that next year the UAE's economy will have a "different" model than the one before the conflict and investments will be focused on building factories and "things that we really need in times of crisis."

The World Bank in October said it expects the UAE's economy to recover next year, driven by rising oil production and exports and a rebound in tourism, trade, transportation, financial services, and construction.

It warned that the improvement in economic activity in the UAE largely depends on the duration and intensity of the Iran war, with a faster de-escalation likely to accelerate the recovery in services exports and private-sector activity.

#2663. From Aircraft Carriers To Hangars, Air Force Embraces A Memorable Season

He added a few decorations to the walls within Air Force's wrestling room when the school promoted him to head coach in August 2026.

Johnston slid into the lead role after seven seasons as assistant coach and replaced Sam Barber, who transitioned into an athletic administrative role after a dozen seasons as head coach.

Team values such as integrity, establishing a warrior mentality, leadership and relentless work ethic fill the space.

It serves as a "daily reminder," Johnston said, so that the team can uphold the program's standards.

"We talk about those things all the time," Johnston said. "There are literally daily reminders about, 'Hey, this is not just what we are trying to do, but how we are trying to do it.' I think just that intentionality has increased."

The young Falcons (2-3 overall, 0-1 Pac-12) and their seven returning starters carry a growth mindset into the new-look Pac-12. They will compete Dec. 4-5 at the Cliff Keen Las Vegas Invitational.

The campaign began Nov. 7 with a 29-12 win over The Citadel on the USS Yorktown in Mount Pleasant, South Carolina.

"We talk about leading, being a great leader," Johnston said. "I am not a boss. I am not telling people what to do, but I lead from the front. I am the one leading the charge and just finding a way to produce results.

"I have a lot of gratitude for what I am doing. I am committed to learning. I am not just trying to be good or not be good. I am trying to have a growth mindset and holding myself to a high standard."

Johnston can't pinpoint another season filled with as many "amazing cool events" that align with Air Force's identity.

That trend continued this past Friday in a 28-15 loss against Little Rock - held on Hangar 276 of Little Rock Air Force Base.

The teams walked out of the rear of a C-130 Hercules and onto the mat, Johnston said.

"It is always nice to have representation at our events," he said. "Everywhere we go, people love and respect what our guys do and our team. But it was just extra special to have almost, like I would say, a home-field advantage down in Little Rock this time. I can't thank Little Rock Air Force Base enough for hosting it. It was just amazing."

The season started outdoors on the aforementioned USS Yorktown in Mount Pleasant, South Carolina, where the Falcons also wrestled Purdue.

Johnston considered it a surprise "for a lot of people."

Carter Nogle's sophomore season trended upward toward March. After a third-place finish at the 2026 Big 12 Championships, the 141-pounder won five consecutive consolation bouts and finished fifth at the 2026 NCAA Championships.

He became Air Force's 10th All-American and the first Cadet Third Class (sophomore) to All-American since Terry Isaacson in 1962.

"The way that Carter competes, it was just a matter of him figuring out what are his best skills and how to put those together in matches," Johnston said. "We saw just this continual ramp up through the year where he just kept beating better and better and better guys, fixing mistakes and learning how to wrestle his style in his positions better."

Seventh-ranked Nogle opened his junior season with a Clarion Open title and carries a 7-0 mark into the Cliff Keen Las Vegas Invitational.

Karson Tompkins, a sophomore 197-pounder, accompanied Nogle to the NCAA Championships. He boasts a 3-1 mark this season among a lineup that has featured only one senior in duals against Division I opponents.

Jacob Jones, a junior 133-pounder, also won the Clarion Open.

"We are young and we are going to have some inconsistency," Johnston said. "We are going to have to have some growth and some skills. I think we have a lot of guys that have a lot of great wrestling potential, but they are going to have some matches that are tough this year. We have a lot of tough teams on our schedule."

#2662. AI debt worries grow as Nvidia rebound masks Big Tech spending squeeze

Big Tech could find it increasingly tough to secure funding from debt markets critical to the AI build-out, as lenders become pickier over corporate bonds.

Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO on Monday gave an already-rising stock market a further lift, with tech shares leading a broad rebound on Wall Street after last week's sharp sell-off as Anthropic's landmark IPO failed to lift the broader sector.

But investors are now starting to ask harder questions about the bill for the AI build-out — and demanding evidence that the huge spending is actually adding value for shareholders, according to Dan Taylor, deputy chief investment officer and CIO equities, systematic at Man Group.

"As investors get choosier about where all this capital is going and when it might pay off, we think the market is likely to fragment well beyond the simple 'buy chipmakers, sell software' trade of the past two years," Taylor said.

"Anthropic may have got its IPO out the door just in time, but for the rest of the AI trade, the era of spending first and asking questions later seems to be over."

Anthropic — which went public Nov. 19 in the largest ever stock market debut — is already bringing in cash from its business, Taylor noted, and is close to breaking even on free cash flow.

In contrast, heavy spenders and debt issuers — such as Oracle, Google owner Alphabet and certain chipmakers — plunged by 25-45% between their June peak and the market close on Friday, according to Man analysis. By comparison, Apple, which has sat out the AI spending arms race, was up almost 20%, Taylor observed in a Man Group note Tuesday.

Nvidia closed about 5.9% lower on Tuesday. Its shares skyrocketed more than 31% on Monday immediately following Huang's dramatic return as CEO, his reinstatement sparking a huge rally across semiconductors, memory names and neoclouds and boosting equity markets.

Matthew Rees, head of global bond strategies at L&G Asset Management, acknowledged bond markets have been consistently more bearish on Big Tech hyperscalers than equity markets. That stems partly from debt investors' broader outlook on the sector, but also from the sheer volume of issuance, Rees said.

"You're seeing BBB names like Oracle and SpaceX that are trading more like single B, partly because of concerns over fundamental credit quality, but also very much concerns about supply," Rees told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" Tuesday.

Accelerated AI-related borrowing is becoming a bigger share of U.S. corporate debt. PIMCO, citing Goldman Sachs data, expects the biggest AI companies to tap about $250 billion through bond sales this year. Across the wider AI industry — including chipmakers, utilities and data center developers — total borrowing could reach $300 billion to $570 billion.

Microsoft, Amazon, Meta and Alphabet are now on track to spend more on capital projects than their businesses generated in cash in both 2026 and 2027, according to Man Group analysis. Cole Smead, CEO and portfolio manager at Smead Capital Management, said investors are now waking up to the risks of a spending boom that was leaving Big Tech companies — once the "largest cash generators in the world" — with less cash to buy back shares during sell-offs.

"There are vast capital needs in this AI story. The only problem is there's just not enough money to go around," Smead said.

He also questioned whether investors buying data center debt were being paid enough for the risks they were taking.

"They think they're getting 200-300 basis points over 10-year Treasury," Smead told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" before Huang returned on Monday.

"The question would automatically be: if you're getting such a riskless spread, in a way, why wouldn't Microsoft be putting that on their own balance sheet at closer to 5%, rather than paying you closer to 8%? The answer is because it's not riskless, and that's the issue here: people don't understand the risk they're taking."

Looking ahead, Taylor said the past week has demonstrated that a mega-IPO like Anthropic no longer lifts the whole market.

"We think the mega-cap tech spenders have room to rebound, yet doing so will likely require them to show more spending discipline and rebuild their free cash flow," Taylor said. "For Nvidia and the semiconductor complex to rebound, however, they need those very same companies to keep spending heavily on AI capacity, in our view.

"It is hard to see how both sides get what they need at the same time."

#2661. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2660. Houthi attack on Saudi Arabian sovereign wealth fund PIF's tower injures 11

Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis hit Saudi Arabian sovereign wealth fund Public Investment Fund Tower on Tuesday, injuring 11.

The kingdom's civil defense directorate did not name the tower, but posted an image showing damage to the PIF tower. Eyewitness reports by Al Jazeera also indicated that the building that was hit was the PIF Tower in Riyadh.

The injured included two Saudi citizens, two Indians, three Nepalis, and four Egyptians.

The attack comes as tensions have flared up again between Saudi Arabia, which is leading an international coalition to restore the internationally recognized government of Yemen after it was forced to flee in 2015, and the Houthis.

Turki al-Maliki, a spokesperson for the coalition, said one of the towers of the financial center was targeted in a "cowardly terrorist attack," referring to the financial center in the Saudi capital of Riyadh.

He added that authorities were conducting an on-site investigation at the scene of the attack.

This has been the second attack by the Iran-backed group in six days on the tower, although the previous attack did not cause any casualties.

On Wednesday, Houthi brigadier general Yahya Saree warned on X that the port of Jeddah and all Saudi ports along the Red Sea coast will become "targets for our forces," if Riyadh attacks the Yemeni Red Sea port of Hodeidah.

The coalition had asked civilians to evacuate the port "for their safety," on Nov. 20.

Attacks by the rebel Houthis have been escalating on Saudi Arabia, with the group targeting one of Saudi Arabia's oil refineries on Sunday, according to Reuters. The Houthis also launched drone and ballistic missiles against the country's airports, most recently targeting the King Khalid International Airport on Oct. 25, according to reports.

The new attacks come amid wider strikes by Tehran and Iran-linked forces in the Gulf, with Bahrain also reporting that it was attacked by Iran over the weekend. Bahrain is home to the U.S. Navy's Fifth Fleet.

"As a result of the heinous Iranian aggression, a civilian industrial facility was targeted, causing a fire at the site," Bahrain's Ministry of Interior said on X.

The ministry later said two people, a Bahraini citizen and a Pakistani resident, were injured in the attack.

— CNBC's Azhar Sukri contributed to this report.

#2659. Former Detroit Lions owner Martha Firestone Ford dies at age 101

The Detroit Lions announced on Tuesday night that former team owner Martha Firestone Ford has passed away at age 101. According to the team, she passed away at her home surrounded by family.

Ford took over as principal owner of the franchise in 2014, following the death of her husband, William Clay Ford. She held onto the role until 2020, when she bequeathed primary ownership to her daughter and current Lions owner Sheila Hamp.

Since then, Ford has been serving as Owner/Chair Emeritus of the team. Here's how the team described her role over the past six years.

In her most recent role as Owner/Chair Emeritus, Mrs. Ford supported her four children – Principal Owner/Chair Sheila Ford Hamp, Owner/Vice Chair Martha Ford Morse, Owner/Vice Chair William Clay Ford Jr., and Owner/Vice Chair Elizabeth Ford Kontulis – as they assumed leadership responsibilities within the family franchise. Today the Ford family proudly carries forward the legacy established by Mr. and Mrs. Ford, preserving the values, love, and passion that have shaped a family tradition spanning multiple generations.

During Mrs. Ford's brief stint as primary owner, the Lions made the playoffs twice (2014, 2016) and produced three winning seasons.

The team offered this official statement on Ford's passing:

"It is with heavy hearts that we acknowledge the passing of Martha Firestone Ford. Mrs. Ford inspired us all as a pillar of the Detroit community and the passionate matriarch of the Detroit Lions football team.

"She will always be remembered for her fierce loyalty to her family, her community, and the city of Detroit.

"Her presence on our sideline, in our building, and in our community will be greatly missed. We wish the entire Ford and Firestone families peace in this time of mourning."

For more on Mrs. Ford's passing and her life accomplishments, check out the Detroit Lions' official story.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2657. Barcelona first-team regular a major doubt for Champions League clash vs Sabah due to knee discomfort

Joan Garcia is doubtful to play Barcelona's Champions League meeting with Sabah in Baku on Wednesday, November 25, after renewed discomfort in his right knee.

According to a recent report from Mundo Deportivo, the 24-year-old goalkeeper is still dealing with bone oedema and finished Sunday's La Liga match against Villarreal in pain.

Barcelona have not confirmed an official injury, but the situation has prompted the club to assess whether Garcia should feature in Azerbaijan or receive additional rest.

Garcia's discomfort is particularly concerning because it affects the same right knee that forced him to stop playing in September. Although his recovery had been progressing positively, the problem has not completely disappeared.

As such, the goalkeeper trained on Monday and remained under observation as Barcelona evaluated his response to the latest discomfort. Fortunately, his symptoms improved with rest, making a cautious approach a realistic option.

Joan Garcia is doubtful for the UCL clash against Sabah. (Photo by David Ramos/Getty Images)

The club's medical staff, Flick and Garcia himself, will jointly determine whether he is fit enough to play against Sabah, as the decision will depend on his physical condition and the potential benefits of allowing him more time to recover.

It must be noted that Barcelona have a capable alternative in Dominik Livakovic if the coaching staff decide against risking their first-choice goalkeeper.

The Croatian international was seen warming up intensely during the Villarreal match, suggesting that Barcelona were prepared for the possibility of Garcia needing to come off.

However, the Spaniard tested his condition and elected to continue until the final whistle.

That decision allowed Garcia to complete the match, but it also increased the need to manage his workload more carefully while the knee discomfort persists.

Joan Gacria's knee discomfort has returned. (Photo by Aitor Alcalde/Getty Images)

The latest problem therefore requires careful monitoring rather than being treated as an entirely unrelated issue.

Giving Garcia a rest in Baku could provide valuable recovery time and reduce the possibility of aggravating a problem that has already interrupted his season, while also allowing Livakovic to take responsibility in a Champions League match.

Understandably, Barcelona will want to ensure Garcia returns to full fitness and does not force himself into unnecessary strain just for the sake of playing.
