#2656. The Shortlist: Real Sociedad gem Jon Martin is well worth €60m punt for Barcelona, Real Madrid and Premier League heavyweights

The talented centre-back has established himself as a key player at the Anoeta, breaking into Spain's senior squad in the process

Real Sociedad have gone to great lengths to secure the future of their most treasured asset over the last year. In October 2025, the Basque club extended Jon Martin's contract through to 2031, and nine months later, they felt compelled to improve his terms again as a host of European giants began circling for his signature.

The 20-year-old extended until 2032, with a reported €60 million (£50m/$66m) release clause added into the contract, and the club said in a glowing official statement: "Jon Martin is the present and the future of the club... a pillar of the Realista backline." That last part is certainly true, but Martin will be a target for the elite once again next year if he continues on his current trajectory, and €60m will be a small price to pay.

According to Sky Sports, "some of the biggest Premier League clubs" are keeping tabs on Martin, who made his senior debut for Spain in a June friendly clash with Iraq. Real Madrid and Barcelona are also being linked with the Sociedad star, but it could easily be argued that his qualities are an even better fit for the English top-flight than La Liga.

La Real have a proud history of producing world-class talent from their academy, with the likes of Antoine Griezmann and Xabi Alonso learning their trade at the Anoeta before becoming superstars elsewhere, and there is every chance that Martin could be their next big success story.

After six years earning his stripes in La Real's academy, Martin got his chance at first-team level in a crucial fixture

Martin was born in Lasarte-Oria near San Sebastian, and took inspiration from his father, Santiago, when taking his first steps into football. Santiago played for La Real between 1897 and 1992, before being forced to retire at just 21 after suffering a severe knee injury.

La Real spotted that the family talent had been passed on in 2018 as they drafted a 12-year-old Martin into their youth ranks. From there, he developed as both a defensive midfielder and centre-back, which shaped the composure he has on the ball these days, and took in a brief loan spell with SD Beasain in 2022.

In early 2023, Martin bypassed traditional age-group steps to make three appearances for Real Sociedad C, before advancing to the B squad ahead of the 2023-24 campaign. The teenager also became captain of Spain's Under-17 team, leading them out at both the U17 European Championship and U17 World Cup.

Sociedad B finished ninth in the Spanish third tier in Martin's sole year with the squad, and he anchored the defence with remarkable composure across 26 appearances, also chipping in with two goals. Martin's aerial dominance in both boxes was crucial to La Real securing a ninth-placed finish, and he earned his senior La Liga debut before the season ended.

Head coach Imanol Alguacil threw Martin on for Kieran Tierney for the final 23 minutes of a clash with Real Betis, and the 18-year-old settled into the backline without a hitch, helping La Real preserve a clean sheet in a 2-0 victory that sealed European qualification.

"I was really happy they thought of me for such an important match," Martin said when reflecting on that moment to Marca. "In a debut you always have that touch of nerves, but once you step onto the pitch, nothing - they disappear."

Despite the uncertainty caused by a revolving door of managers, Martin continued to go from strength to strength

Martin was integrated permanently into the first-team squad for the 2024-25 season, and made 17 appearances across all competitions as La Real reached the semi-finals of the Copa del Rey and last-16 of the Europa League. They did, however, only manage an 11th-placed finish in La Liga, and Alguacil decided to step down after six years in charge.

He was replaced by Sociedad B coach Sergio Francisco, who already had a strong bond with Martin, and though the youngster found himself behind Duje Caleta-Car in the early months of Franciso's reign and suffered a frustrating injury setback, the manager eventually made Martin the centrepiece of his defence upon his return in October.

Martin excelled in an initial four-match unbeaten run, earning widespread plaudits for his distribution and assured one-vs-one defending, but La Real then lost three matches in a row to sit just one point above the relegation zone by mid-December, and Francisco was relieved of his duties.

Losing such a close ally was a blow for Martin, but Francisco's successor, Pellegrino Matarazzo, also took an immediate liking to the Spanish centre-half. Matarazzo immediately lifted the squad's confidence levels, winning four and drawing three of his first seven league games, with a 2-1 home triumph over Barcelona the highlight as Martin led a gruelling low-block effort to frustrate the likes of Lamine Yamal and Fermin Lopez.

Most pundits put Barca's failure down to bad luck on the night, but Matarazzo was quick to point out how much work Martin and his team-mates put in, telling reporters: "Being alert is not luck. Throwing yourself in front of the ball is not luck - it's being aware of the moment."

Martin was one of La Real's standout players in their stunning Copa del Rey final triumph over Atletico Madrid

Matarazzo managed to drag his team back up to 10th in La Liga, which would have been enough to justify his appointment alone, but the American tactician also immortalised himself at the Anoeta by delivering the club's fourth Copa del Rey trophy. La Real edged out Osasuna on penalties in the round of 16, beat Alaves 3-2 in the quarter finals, and overcame bitter rivals Athletic Club in a Basque derby semi-final tie, winning both legs 1-0, to set up a blockbuster final meeting with Atletico Madrid.

After a 2-2 draw across normal and extra-time in Seville, La Real prevailed 4-3 in a tense penalty shootout. Martin produced a colossal display with nine duel wins, 13 clearances and 17 ball carries, also completing 92 percent of passes in his own half.

"Jon Martin is a fighter. He settled into the match. After the first 15 minutes, he played a fantastic game," Matarazzo said in his post-match press-conference. "He is a very important player for us because he brings a lot of emotion, a lot of fighting spirit. He can ignite the fans. I think he had a great game today."

It was no surprise that Luis de la Fuente drafted Martin into the Spain squad after that, and he was unlucky not to make the cut for the 2026 World Cup or the manager's first two squads of the current season. Dean Huijsen is in pole position to become Pau Cubarsi's long-term partner in the heart of the Roja defence, with Aymeric Laporte entering the latter stages of his career at 32, but De la Fuente won't be able to ignore Martin if he keeps progressing so rapidly.

To unlock the full scope of his talent, Martin would be best served by leaving his La Liga comfort zone

Indeed, Martin has been one of the best defenders in La Liga during the opening four months of the 2026-27 campaign. He ranks in the top five percent among all centre-backs in Europe's top five leagues for aerial duel and general duel win rate, and is now the first name on Matarazzo's teamsheet.

Sociedad have broken into La Liga's top five, four points clear of Sevilla in the race for what could prove to be a Champions League qualification spot, while they have also been billed as dark horses for the Europa League. With only five points from four games in the league phase, La Real have lacked a killer edge in Europe, but there were significant signs of improvement in a 1-1 draw against Lyon last time out.

Martin is already up to 15 starts in all competitions and his continued presence is essential as La Real strive to win more silverware and qualify for the Champions League. If they achieve either of those objectives, there's a good chance Martin will be in extremely high demand next summer.

In October, he told Sky Sports that "everything I need to be able to perform at a top level is here at this club", but also admitted he is grateful for the interest he's receiving, adding: "I think that I am a player people like." That should give his suitors a glimmer of hope.

Arsenal, Chelsea and Liverpool have all been named on that ever-growing list, with the latter perhaps the most attractive potential destination. As a press-resistant defender comfortable dribbling out of danger and breaking the lines with his passing, Martin would be ideally suited to fellow San Sebastian native Andoni Iraola's transition-heavy 4-2-3-1 system.

Martin is tailor-made for the higher tempo and more set-piece driven style of Premier League football, too, and would be a major weapon in both penalty areas. Of course, it would be a much easier transition if he were to pick Barca or Madrid, but it feels like he is already close to his ceiling in La Liga.

Liverpool would be wise to get ahead of the competition before Martin's value increases. Whatever happens, though, he's got an extremely bright future ahead of him.

#2655. Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Tuesday struck a conciliatory and remorseful tone while leading his first employee meeting since being reinstated, and said how sorry he was for what he had done.

Huang told employees that he let them down and promised to do a better job the second time around, according to people inside the room, who asked not to be named in order to share details. "I hurt all of you," Huang said, according to one of the people.

The CEO also addressed succession planning during the event, the people said. He told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.

A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to one of the people present.

Investors and analysts were left to speculate about the company's future leadership in the wake of Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.

On Monday, Huang was reinstated as CEO of the chipmaker by Nvidia's board of directors. Eleven days before, Huang had resigned from Nvidia after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said last week that Huang resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

Huang started the meeting by thanking CFO Colette Kress for her leadership as interim CEO.

Huang told staff that Tuesday's company meeting was the second hardest meeting of his life, the first one being with his wife. He said he is in therapy, according to one of the people present.

In a press release announcing Huang's return on Monday, the company said the board "received input" from senior officials in the federal government about any potential national security risk around the CEO. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he gave the board assurances and "made the deal" to restore Huang to his post.

After the company had its worst week in the stock market since 2008, the stock surged more than 30% the day his return was announced. Shares fell on Tuesday, however.

An Nvidia representative didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

The Pro Football Hall of Fame announced the names of 52 players who have advanced to the next round of voting for possible enshrinement as part of the Class of 2027.

The group includes five players who are in their first year of eligibility. They are tight end Rob Gronkowski, running back Adrian Peterson, quarterback Ben Roethlisberger, wide receiver Antonio Brown, and cornerback Richard Sherman.

After changes to the selection process this year, there are no longer Modern and Senior candidates in the player pool. The group of 52 players will be cut to 25 in the next round of voting and then 13 finalists will be selected from that group. They will join Bill Belichick and Robert Kraft in the final pool of eligible candidates with the selection committee selecting a group of between three and seven members.

#2650. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2648. Colts coaches weigh in on Anthony Richardson’s abrupt return to the starting lineup

As long as he can stay healthy, he projects to start these dismantled 3-8 Colts' final six games down the stretch of the 2026 campaign.

The former 4th overall pick of the 2023 NFL Draft has only started 15 games since being selected by Indianapolis, and his last start for the Colts occurred on December 22, 2024, against the Tennessee Titans. #2646. Sixers' record-setting wait for the West ends against Trail Blazers

Sixers' record-setting wait for the West ends against Trail Blazers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Have you noticed anything different about the Sixers' season?

No, not the fact that LeBron James and Jaylen Brown are wearing red, white and blue and have helped Philadelphia build an Eastern Conference-leading 14-3 record.

The difference is hidden in their schedule. All 17 games have come against Eastern Conference opponents.

When the Sixers welcome the Portland Trail Blazers to Xfinity Mobile Arena on Monday night, it will mark the latest they have ever gone into a season before facing a Western Conference team.

Philadelphia's 18-game wait also ties the 1979-80 Atlanta Hawks for the second latest by an Eastern Conference team. Only the 1980-81 Milwaukee Bucks went longer, meeting the West for the first time in their 21st game.

The Sixers have played who has been placed in front of them and won five consecutive games. They have already beaten the Knicks twice and own victories over Cleveland and Detroit.

Their first Western Conference matchup introduces a different comparison.

The West entered Monday with 11 teams above .500. The East had seven. Western Conference teams were also 41-20 against the East through Sunday.

Portland will not answer every question about how the Sixers compare with the other conference. It begins a more varied portion of their schedule.

The Sixers will face Memphis and Denver on consecutive nights next week. Dallas and Houston visit later in December before Philadelphia travels to play the Lakers, Clippers, Kings and Suns over a six-day stretch.

Portland is 10-6 under first-year head coach Micah Nori, the father of Phillies prospect Dante Nori.

The Blazers are averaging 116.1 points while allowing 110.3 per game, with both figures ranking in the top 10. Their 15.4 made three-pointers per game rank second in the NBA.

Deni Avdija leads Portland with 23.9 points per game. Damian Lillard is averaging 20.6 points in his return to the franchise, while 7-foot-2 center Donovan Clingan has supplied size around a deep group of guards and wings.

Clingan posted 22 points and 13 rebounds Saturday night at Madison Square Garden. Portland erased a 26-point deficit and briefly took the lead before Mikal Bridges made a game-winning three-pointer at the buzzer.

The 97-96 loss dropped the Blazers to 1-3 over their last four games. Slow starts have followed them during that stretch.

"Our last three games, we haven't gotten off to great starts, and that's just something that we got to correct," Lillard said. "Our energy and our focus and our intent has to be better from the jump. We can't let this trend continue."

The Sixers know something about overcoming early deficits. Six of their 14 victories have required double-digit comebacks.

Portland will be missing two major pieces. Ja Morant is out with a left hamstring strain, while Shaedon Sharpe remains sidelined following right meniscus surgery.

Even without Morant, the Blazers can turn to Lillard, Jrue Holiday and Scoot Henderson to initiate their offense. Avdija and Toumani Camara provide size on the perimeter, while Clingan gives Portland the kind of interior presence that has occasionally troubled the Sixers.

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker know the Portland organization better than any other Sixers.

The two shared the floor for 128 games with the Trail Blazers. Portland won 43 of them.

Their responsibilities have changed in Philadelphia. So have the expectations.

Simons scored 18 points in each of the Sixers' last two games and made seven three-pointers. He started in place of Tyrese Maxey against Orlando and hit the clinching three in his hometown. Three nights later, he supplied four more threes off the bench in the Sixers' 132-129 win over Milwaukee.

Walker has also found steady work during the Sixers' recent frontcourt injuries. He recorded nine points, seven rebounds and four assists against the Bucks after scoring 11 against Indiana and hitting a go-ahead three late in Orlando.

Both players recently discussed how their difficult seasons in Portland changed their perspective on filling smaller roles for a winning team.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something that you can't take for granted, for sure, because it's hard to win in the NBA. We've seen it firsthand."

Their former team will now see them operating within a deeper rotation than the one Philadelphia carried through much of the past week.

Brown, Dean Wade and Adem Bona were all listed as probable on the Sixers' initial injury report after missing time.

Brown and Wade have each missed the last two games. Bona has been sidelined since Nov. 4 because of a cervical spine sprain. Dominick Barlow returned Saturday and scored 12 points on 5-for-6 shooting in his season debut.

Joel Embiid was added to the injury report Monday morning as questionable because of an illness. His availability will determine how the Sixers approach Clingan and Robert Williams III inside.

The Sixers have leaned on smaller lineups and reserve contributors while Nurse has managed injuries across the roster. Monday could give him more options, even if Embiid cannot play.

FanDuel Sportsbook listed the Sixers as 3.5-point favorites Monday afternoon. Philadelphia was -152 on the moneyline, Portland was +128 and the over-under was 229.5.

The Blazers are 7-4 on the road. The Sixers are 7-2 at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

After 17 games within their own conference, the Sixers will finally see what their early success looks like against the other side.

#2645. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2644. Titans have only defense in the NFL giving up scores on most of its drives

The Titans’ defense allowed the Cowboys’ offense to score on every drive on Sunday, with the exception of a kneeldown at the end of the game. That put the Titans into some very bad territory.

Tennessee’s defense has now given up a score on 50.5 percent of opposing possessions this season. The Titans are the only defense in the league that gives up points most of the time it takes the field.

Over the last two games, the Titans’ defense went on an incredible run: In addition to giving up scores on the Cowboys’ first eight offensive possessions in Week 11, the Titans gave up points on the Jaguars’ last five possessions in Week 10. That streak of 13 consecutive drives giving up scores is the longest streak of consecutive drives in which any NFL defense has given up points, for as far back as full drive data is available.

The Titans’ defense’s last 14 drives ended in 10 touchdowns, three field goals and a kneeldown.

Titans coach Robert Saleh has had success as a defensive coordinator, but he went 20-36 in his previous stint as head coach of the Jets, and he’s now 1-9 with the Titans. When he’s the head coach, his defenses don’t get the job done.

LeBron Log No. 6: A Rocky night, a shooting lesson and a fight over 'Bron' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Everyone looked like exactly who they were portraying. That is Hollywood for you. It was pretty cool."

The Sixers shared photos of James alongside Jaylen Brown, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, Labaron Philon Jr., Jabari Walker, Tacko Fall and Adem Bona.

"Movie night, courtesy of LeBron," the team wrote.

The nationwide release of I Play Rocky came less than two weeks before Philadelphia celebrates the 50th anniversary of the original film on Dec. 3.

Nick Nurse could not attend because of a previous commitment, but he received positive reviews from his players.

"They went, they said it was very good, they loved it," Nurse said. "The big takeaway was how much Anthony actually looked like Rocky. Or Sylvester Stallone. One in the same."

James' first season in Philadelphia now includes a private Rocky screening. A trip up the Art Museum steps remains available for a future edition.

James and Steve Nash devoted the latest episode of their Mind the Game podcast to the art of shooting.

The two examined shooting form, confidence, repetitions, free throws and some of their favorite jumpers. James also revealed one detail that can affect what he does after catching the ball.

"I'm a guy who don't like shooting against the laces," James said. "So sometimes I'll come up on a quick stop and I'm about to raise and I can feel the laces not fixed. I'll turn that into a pump fake."

The episode was released Nov. 17, while James was experiencing an extended struggle from three-point range. It did not provide an instant fix.

James went 1 for 5 from three against Orlando and missed his lone attempt against Milwaukee. He entered Monday night's meeting with Portland shooting 21.7 percent from deep through his first 13 games.

James made all three of his first-half attempts from three. He ended the second quarter by pump-faking, stepping to his left and beating the buzzer with a corner three that tied the game at 69.

Nobody could see where the laces sat on the catch. The pump fake was clear.

James finished 4 for 5 from three and scored a season-high 23 points on 8-for-13 shooting. It was his third consecutive game with at least 20 points, following 22 against Orlando and 21 against Milwaukee.

The Sixers lost to Portland, 128-111, but James produced his best long-range performance since joining them.

Six days after discussing the art of shooting, he provided a brief demonstration.

James is also attempting to protect one of his most recognizable nicknames.

James and LBJ Trademarks LLC filed a federal lawsuit on Nov. 17 against PlayVision Inc. and its founders over a sports-betting research app initially called "Bron."

The complaint alleges that the company used an AI-generated version of James in an advertisement without his permission. The figure wore a No. 23 jersey with "Bron" across the back and resembled James through its voice, build and tattoos.

It also scrolled through wagers involving NBA players.

The lawsuit alleges that the advertisement falsely created the impression that James endorsed the app and suggested that he considered betting on NBA games. The complaint said the video received roughly 1 million views before it was removed.

"The integrity of NBA competition, and the public's confidence that players are not betting on the games they play, is of paramount importance to the league, its players and its fans," the complaint said. "Any suggestion that an active NBA player is betting on NBA games is therefore far from a trivial matter, and it should not be used for clicks and views."

The app offered odds comparisons, betting trends and tracking tools but did not directly accept wagers, according to its developer.

PlayVision renamed it "Evie" on Nov. 18, one day after James filed the lawsuit. James is seeking an injunction and unspecified damages. The case remains active in the Southern District of New York.

CLEVELAND – NOVEMBER 25: LeBron James #23 of the Cleveland Cavaliers looks to pass off when double teamed by the Philadelphia 76ers November 25, 2006 at The Quicken Loans Arena in Cleveland, Ohio. Copyright 2006 NBAE (Photo by David Liam Kyle/NBAE via Getty Images)

James spent Thanksgiving weekend in 2006 on the other side of the Sixers-Cavaliers matchup.

On Nov. 25, 2006, a 21-year-old James recorded 25 points, 11 assists, four rebounds and one block in Cleveland's 108-95 win over Philadelphia.

Donning the Cavs' old-school orange threads, he played nearly 41 minutes.

Iverson led the Sixers with 31 points. Andre Iguodala added 17 points and eight rebounds, while Kyle Korver made three three-pointers off the bench.

James recorded 11 assists that night. Nearly 20 years later, another 11-assist performance against Indiana moved him past Jason Kidd for third place in NBA history.

The 2006 meeting also came near the end of Iverson's original run with Philadelphia. The Sixers traded him to Denver less than a month later.

James was 21. Iverson was nearing his departure.

Twenty years later, James is wearing a Sixers uniform and Iverson's No. 3 hangs above his home floor.

#2642. Chicago Bears Pass Protection Analysis on the Sacks Allowed vs Saints

It's a short week with the Chicago Bears playing on Thursday, and I have a lot to do before kickoff, so let's get right into the two sacks allowed against the New Orleans Saints.

Sack 21: 2nd Quarter: 1-10-NO 14 (12:40) (Shotgun) C.Williams sacked at NO 19 for -5 yards (C.Wooden)

The Saints dropped seven into coverage, and they had everything locked up. Caleb Williams worked left to right, and you could see him through his progressions. He got through at least 4 and had nowhere to go before he started to feel the pocket get tight. He tried to sneak up through the middle, but defensive lineman Colby Wooden got him.

There's less space to operate in the red zone, and things happen quicker, so he didn't have time to get to his fifth read and D'Andre Swift in the right flat. Even if he had, a defender was coming down there too to take that away.

Sometimes an offense has to tip their cap and acknowledge a good play by the defense, and sometimes sacks happen.

Sack 22: 3rd Quarter: 2-6-CHI 37 (11:35) (Shotgun) C.Williams sacked at CHI 26 for -11 yards (J.Sanker)

"The one I can think of was an empty play where we were hot with the nickel coming from the field there. We had a hot answer that we felt like the ball could have come out to on time. That's just making sure we're dialed into it."

#2638. Tommy DeVito embracing latest chance to be NFL starting QB

"Tommy Cutlets" has been in this spot before.

Former Giants quarterback Tommy DeVito, who became a household name in New York, is getting another chance to be in the pressure cooker as a starting signal caller.

This time his task is to replace the injured Drake Maye, who suffered a fractured right fibula in the Patriots' blowout loss to the Lions in Germany on Nov. 15.

Maye is expected to be out at least six weeks, and though head coach Mike Vrabel hopes Maye can return at some point of the season, it's no guarantee he will be ready to return for New England in 2026.

Tommy DeVito, who is replacing injured starter Drake Maye, is all smiles as he talks to reporters during a news conference before the Patriots' practice on Nov. 24, 2026, in Foxborough, Mass. AP

Either way, the chances of the Patriots (5-5), who are coming off a bye, to make the playoffs will hinge on how well DeVito plays, and that starts Sunday with a road test against the 1-9 Chargers, who suffered a loss to the Jets this past week.

"Everybody has embraced me from the first snap and first play I had last week (in the loss to Detroit)," DeVito told reporters, referring to an outing in which he struggled, completing just 12 of 24 for 81 yards with one interception and a fourth-quarter touchdown pass to Lan Larison. "I've put my best foot forward in trying to earn their trust."

During the 2023 season with the Giants, DeVito, a northern New Jersey native, showed he can provide a spark when he was forced into the lineup after Daniel Jones and Tyrod Taylor suffered injuries.

In that six-game span, "Tommy Cutlets" led Big Blue to three straight victories in which he became the first undrafted free agent rookie since 1970 to have three consecutive games with a passer rating higher than 100. But he couldn't sustain the strong play and was benched again by Brian Daboll for a once-again healthy Taylor during a loss to the Eagles later in the season.

DeVito, who is 3-5 as a starter, started two games for Big Blue in the 2024 season after the Giants benched and waived Jones, but an arm injury and concussion sent him to the sidelines as Drew Lock started the final six games.

He became the odd man out after the Giants signed veterans Russell Wilson and Jameis Winston and following the selection of Jaxson Dart in the first round of the 2025 draft.

The Giants cut DeVito that summer but hoped to re-sign him to the practice squad after he cleared waivers. Instead, he was claimed by the Patriots.

DeVito spent the 2025 season on the practice squad behind Maye and Josh Dobbs but then supplanted Dobbs as Maye's backup when Dobbs was released. He signed a two-year extension worth up to $7.4 million with $2 million guaranteed.

Tommy DeVito throws a pass during the Patriots' Week 10 loss to the Lions in Germany. Kirby Lee-Imagn Images

Vrabel knows the intangible spark DeVito can bring to a team, but says the time he had working with the Patriots' wide receivers during training camp will benefit him, as well as the starters' reps he has received this week.

"A lot of energy. I think a lot of work in there with those guys," Vrabel said. "I think he's taken advantage of his opportunities. I think he's also improved, just fundamentally. … Those things probably didn't show up with the exposure from his last game and when he got into it. But I think being able to see some of these things, work on them, focus on them and understand what it is that he can do a little bit better, I think that that'll obviously help him and in turn, help us.″

DeVito — who said offensive coordinator Josh McDaniels and quarterbacks coach Ashton Grant "want me to be me" — is pumped up for his latest chance to lead a team as a starter.

"You feel more energized for sure. It's time to go. Injuries are part of the game, unfortunately. For the players that are in that position, that's how you get opportunities," DeVito said.

#2636. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2633. Trevor Connelly enters Calder conversation in Golden Knights’ win over Edmonton

EDMONTON—It is almost shocking that Trevor Connelly is not discussed more often when discussing favorites for the Calder Trophy.

While there are certainly deserving candidates, Connelly has consistently stayed at or near the top among all rookies in goals and points. Even on a contending team like the Vegas Golden Knights have been year in and year out, Connelly has gone from the prospect Kelly McCrimmon refused to move to one of the most electrifying weapons the team has in their arsenal. Head coach Ryan Craig has already placed an immense amount of trust in Connelly since their days together in Henderson, and the rookie has done nothing but earn every bit of the praise he has received.

With the Golden Knights in the only game of the night on Tuesday, Connelly introduced himself on the national stage with a convincing effort in his first foray against the Edmonton Oilers, as the Golden Knights battled to a 5-3 victory at Rogers Place.

Early on, the Oilers looked the part of a team returning home from a six-game road trip.

A high sticking penalty from Oilers defenseman Mattias Ekholm would give the Golden Knights a double minor to work with, and the power play would ultimately take advantage. Jack Eichel would land a perfectly placed shot from the left faceoff dot to the far post, beating goaltender Devon Levi to break the ice.

Mitch Marner's primary assist would be the 600th of his career, while Victor Olofsson would continue his point-per-game pace on the secondary helper with his 13th point in as many games.

Marner would continue to make an impact in the first period, pressuring Shakir Mukhamadullin into a turnover directly to Connelly. The rookie would then skate unopposed to the front of the net and beat Levi with an impressive backhand shot to give the Golden Knights some breathing room.

The Oilers thought they had cut the lead back to one when Vasily Podkolzin's shot sneaked past the goal line, but it was determined that Mathieu Joseph knocked Carter Hart's stick out of his hand to trigger goaltender interference.

That play would ultimately wake the Oilers up from their first-period malaise. First, Evan Bouchard would unleash a rocket from the point that shattered Colton Dach's stick on its way past Hart. While the Vegas goaltender would keep his team in the lead, the dam would finally break after Jeremy Lauzon's broken stick would give Connor McDavid enough space to tie the game with seconds to go in the middle frame.

Even more concerning, however, was Hart being slow to get up after attempting to stop McDavid. Adin Hill would replace him to finish the period, but Hart would return to close out the game.

With both teams dominating one period, the Golden Knights would come out firing. Nic Dowd would begin with his first goal of the season with a close-range shot, followed by Eichel finishing an odd-man rush with Connelly to reclaim a two-goal lead.

Podkolzin would score seconds after Eichel to cut down the lead, but the Golden Knights remained resilient to hold things down until Connelly scored the empty net goal for his first three-point outing in the NHL.

The game pushed Connelly to 19 points on the year, overtaking Chicago Blackhawks forward Roman Kantserov for the lead among all rookies.

The Golden Knights will return to Nevada to celebrate Thanksgiving with their families, then take to the road once again for a Black Friday afternoon matinee against the San Jose Sharks.

#2631. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2620. Kalshi daily volume of ‘perps’ trading begins to top its prediction markets

Kalshi is known for its prediction markets across sports, politics and culture.

But now, for the first time, perpetual futures, Kalshi's second financial product, has surpassed the notional trading volume of its event contracts on consecutive days.

On Thursday, perpetual futures — commonly known as "perps" — on Kalshi saw $3.4 billion in notional volume as artificial intelligence laboratory Anthropic stock debuted on public markets while the rest of the market sold off. That exceeded about $3 billion in notional volume for Kalshi's prediction markets on the same day.

Perps are futures-style contracts where there is no expiration and traders do not need to own the underlying asset. Contracts instead continuously track the price of an asset, with funding payments to keep the contract price aligned with that of the market. Funding payments are made periodically between holders of short or long positions.

Perps trading surpassed event contracts one day in September, but on Friday perps topped prediction markets volume for a second day. Now Kalshi expects that performance to continue as it introduces perps tied to more individual stocks. On Monday, for example, Kalshi launched contracts linked to Anthropic.

Kalshi was the first to debut perps in the U.S. after winning federal regulatory approval in May to list perps tied to cryptocurrencies.

Perps trading volume includes any leverage that speculators acquire along with their trade, while event contracts are fully collateralized — for now.

Since the original launch in May, Kalshi has received approval to introduce perps tied to metals, crude oil, stock indexes and individual stocks.

Offshore perps — those available on exchanges not regulated in the U.S. — generated more than $90 trillion in notional trading volume in 2025, so it's not surprising that the product has grown so rapidly on Kalshi, according to Udesh Jha, chief risk officer at Kalshi Klear, the company's clearing house.

"We're just in very early innings. We're launching products, we're growing the participant base."

Perps were Kalshi's first product to mark its transition from a prediction market-focused platform to a full-blown financial exchange where various assets are traded. Last week, reports said that Kalshi is planning to offer trading on equities on its platform in the coming months.

At the same time, the company's perps product has come under fire. Some incumbent exchanges where futures are traded — which saw their stock prices decline following the approval of U.S. perps — believe the product was improperly approved. CME Group is suing the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal derivatives regulator, for approving perps.

Others have simply expressed concerns about the efficiency of the markets. CNBC previously reported that trading volume on Kalshi's most popular perps markets — tied to bitcoin and ether cryptocurrencies — was dramatically greater than open interest, or cash actively in the markets. It's a phenomenon that isn't common with U.S.-regulated traditional futures, nor other offshore perps markets.

Kalshi said at the time it has "zero concerns" about the distinction, and that it appears because less leverage is offered compared to offshore exchanges and there are fewer incentives for traders to park capital on its perps markets.

While Kalshi started with cryptocurrencies, Owen Lau, an analyst at Clear Street, said perps tied to individual stocks likely represent the most promising opportunity for growth in the U.S.

"That's how I foresee the way perps will penetrate further into traditional asset classes," Lau said. "Perps on single stocks and [key performance indicators on individual companies] could change how investors hedge their portfolio or even make a directional bet."

Kalshi's growth has been powered by the fact that right now it's the only exchange that can actively list perps in the U.S., though crypto exchange Coinbase received approval in May to route U.S. users to offshore platforms for trading. That monopoly status, however, will probably prove short lived.

Coinbase has filed to list single-stock perps in the U.S., and President Donald Trump in August said the CFTC is working to bring Hyperliquid — a dominant player in offshore perps activity — to domestic traders.

But Jha at Kalshi isn't worried about the competition.

"What will carry us is ... making sure that we have the right products for the marketplace, making sure we have the right surveillance of the products that we are trading."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2617. AI debt markets shudder as Nvidia's top 'banker' returns

AI credit markets are shuddering again with Jensen Huang back as CEO of Nvidia.

Fears about the massive debt being taken on to fund the artificial intelligence buildout climbed Tuesday as Wall Street weighed what Huang's return to Nvidia means for its bankrolling of the AI boom.

Huang is considered one of the top financiers, or bankers, in the AI industry, spearheading deals with neoclouds like CoreWeave and other firms to buy chips, backed in some cases by Nvidia guarantees. His return to Nvidia has revived prospects for the company to continue "circular financing" of AI chips and data centers — raising concerns among credit investors.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child of AI debt concerns, saw its stock sink 13% and the yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 top 17%. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

At the same time, the spread on the neocloud company's five-year credit default swaps jumped 8.5% to over 1,200 basis points. Credit default swaps are insurance against nonpayment and are considered a barometer of perceived risks. Rising CDS spreads mean investors view the debt as riskier.

Huang was reinstated as CEO on Monday after stepping down on Nov. 12 amid allegations of multiple affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. His reinstatement on Monday was cheered by investors, who pushed stocks higher, yields lower and spreads on CDS tighter. However, stocks retreated, bond yields rose and CDS spreads widened on Tuesday.

Ben Emons, founder and chief investment officer at FedWatch Advisors, called it a waning of the "Jensen effect."

The moves suggest "markets see government intervention as 'moral suasion' that carries credit and interest-rate risks," he said.

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday he "convinced" the board to bring Huang back.

CoreWeave wasn't the only name seeing heightened concerns in CDS spreads. Oracle's five-year CDS spread jumped 7% to 355.68. Even highly rated hyperscalers Meta Platforms and Amazon.com saw spreads widen by more than 2%. Nvidia's CDS added 3.4%.

Larry Holzenthaler, senior portfolio manager for fixed income at Catalyst Funds, believes the drama that unfolded with Huang's return to Nvidia won't necessarily sit well with credit investors.

"A lot of old school credit people are going to look at what kind of materialized there, the administration's involvement in putting him back in, and I think a lot of credit participants are going to step back and tiptoe away from that a bit," he said. "Credit market investors typically don't like that type of drama or uncertainty."

While Nvidia has commitments across the AI ecosystem, it is the neoclouds that have been under the most scrutiny. Many of the companies, which rent out AI computing power and cloud capacity, are highly leveraged and have relied on Nvidia financing to reduce their exposure to the bond market.

With so much riding on Nvidia's funding, Huang's next move is being closely watched.

During his brief absence, chief financial officer Colette Kress said during the company's earnings call that Nvidia would be "rigorously evaluating future investments" and will remain disciplined in how it commits capital.

That suggested Nvidia was going to be more judicious with financing, says Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"That concern should be put to rest with Jensen [Huang] back," he said.

Investor Steve Eisman, of "Big Short" fame and host of "The Real Eisman Playbook" podcast, believes Huang feels emboldened now that he's back in charge.

"[H]e'll double down on the circular financing," Eisman predicted on CNBC's "Squawk Box" Tuesday.

After Huang's departure, Eisman believed that the ecosystem the CEO created by pouring money into AI companies would likely unwind and leave some of the companies in "big trouble."

"Now that he's back, they're not," he said.

Others are not so certain the level of funding will continue.

"Given scrutiny of disclosure around financing and guarantees … we do not expect Mr. Huang's return to bring a quick reversion to broader customer financing," Stifel analyst Ruben Roy said in a note Monday.

Nvidia said on its earnings call that it has $118 billion in guarantees, as of Oct. 25, to back AI projects.

Concerns about the level of debt to fund the AI infrastructure buildout had already taken hold before Huang's departure and reinstatement.

"These issues have not disappeared just because the White House pressured the weak board to reinstate Jensen Huang," said economist David Rosenberg, founder and president of Rosenberg Research.

John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson Investors, believes having Huang back at the helm "reaffirms his existing go-to-market strategy." However, the credit market is still taking a cautious look at the neoclouds as it waits for more clarity on long-term supply/demand dynamics.

Still, Lloyd is cautiously positive on CoreWeave, which he owns in portfolios he manages, and noted all the data point to a "pretty healthy environment right now." That includes strong residual values on graphics processing units and spot compute rates, he noted.

"The company is doing everything in its power to execute," he said. "They don't need the high yield market at this time."

In the meantime, investigations into Nvidia and Huang are ramping up. Huang is expected to appear before three House committees next week. Nvidia is conducting an independent investigation into his activities, and California Attorney General Rob Bonta has launched a probe. At the federal level, President Trump said Monday that U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche "has confirmed that the U.S. Government does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"There's other shoes to drop," Rosenberg said. "I don't think that the book has been closed on this file."

#2615. Game Preview #19: Wolves at Thunder Thread

Well, if it isn't our old friends the Oklahoma City Thunder? They've been suffering through their second straight year of heavy injuries, but the Wolves aren't the shoulder to cry on. One team has managed to reshape their roster and stay afloat despite losing their best player and other rotation to various ailments, the other is barely staying afloat while dealing with their own litany of players sidelined.

Let's see what we got cooking on Thanksgiving eve.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (13-5) at Oklahoma City Thunder (10-8)

When: Wednesday, November 25 at 6:30 PM CT

The back-to-back reigning Most Valuable Player, Shai Gilgeous-Alexander, is number two league-wide in total free throws attempted. Sixth in total fouls drawn. I'm just listing that here for no particular reason.

SGA is also having his least efficient scoring season since the 2021-22 campaign. His three-point shooting volume is up, but it shouldn't be surprising to see his two-point percentage come down. It's because he hasn't been flanked by three of his starters: Jalen Williams suffered a gruesome leg injury, Cason Wallace tore his shoulder labrum, and Chet Holmgren has a tear in his knee. Many of these are long-term, if not season-ending, ailments.

The Thunder are finally feeling fear for the first time in the regular season in a while.

Shai has been forced to carry his team more than he's ever had to. His .358 usage percentage is second in the league behind just Luka Dončić. He's felt it, quite literally. He had to miss a game on Sunday due to a neck/back issue. Will he be at 100% against the Wolves on Wednesday? The Thunder better hope so.

Oklahoma City has long been known for their incredible depth (and collection of future draft picks). In fact, they were so rich in depth that they were paying to get off of rotation role players like Lu Dort, Isaiah Joe, and Aaron Wiggins. Had they known their starters were going to get bitten by the injury bug so badly, they'd probably undo some of those decisions. They're currently leaning heavily on holdovers like Isaiah Hartenstein and Kenrich Williams.

Until then, they've had some rookies and unfamiliar faces step up. You may recognize the pair of 2024 rookies in Ajay Mitchell and Jared McCain, who are the second and third leading scorers on the team. Additionally, their 2026 rookies have been a pleasant surprise for them. Mara has been force in the paint on both ends of the court, doing his best Holmgren impression. He stands at 7'2" and has a wingspan longer than Rocco Zikarsky (but shorter than Rudy's). Meanwhile, Stirtz has come on as of late, becoming a legitimate perimeter scoring threat.

Thunder general manager Sam Presti know how to pick 'em. Minnesota should be wise to not get caught sleeping on 'em.

This is a very casual observation, but the Thunder currently go only as far as Shai can take them. Their turnover rate is so low because they rely so much on isolation, particularly from SGA. They literally have to hope he can muster up enough points to carry them to a slim margin of victory.

In Oklahoma City's past eight games, they've won just two of them. The opponents were the Sacramento Kings and New Orleans Pelicans. Shai had to score 37 or more to even defeat those bottom-feeders (respectfully).

Minnesota has no shortage of defenders they can throw at him. The Thunder are a fairly small team (they just started Micah Peavy, a 6'7" guard, at power forward) so that should allow for guys Jaden McDaniels and Jonathan Kuminga to get opportunities to stop the Canadian.

#2614. Michigan does about-face on Trev Alberts, potentially owing him $17.2 million

The last time Trev Alberts made this kind of a stir, he was the subject of Bill Tobin’s legendary rant against Mel Kiper.

This time, the situation potentially involves a significant payment to Alberts.

Via ESPN, Michigan backed out of an agreement to hire Alberts (who played in only 29 career games over three seasons after being the fifth overall pick in the 1994 draft) to become the university’s next athletic director. Alberts told ESPN that he had signed a “binding memorandum of understanding” for the position.

Alberts’s lawyer, Tom Mars, told ESPN that Michigan owes Alberts a $17.2 million buyout.

For that kind of money, Michigan will be very tempted to refuse to pay, forcing litigation and the expense, uncertainty, and delay that goes along with it. Alberts could decide to take less than he’s owed, simply to end the fight.

Of course, Alberts also could become determined to prove a point — if he and Mars believe Alberts has an ironclad claim to the money.

Michigan’s about-face apparently relates to deposition testimony from Alberts in a Title IX case arising from his tenure at Nebraska. Per ESPN, Alberts gave testimony on December 4, 2025, that Michigan regards as problematic.

Alberts was asked whether he believes it’s true that college coaches should never have sex with the athletes they’re coaching. The obvious answer is, “Yes.” Alberts pursued a different strategy.

“I don’t know the answer to that,” Alberts said.

“Well, I mean, you know, it just depends,” Alberts replied.

He then said the situation could be “nuanced,” due to preexisting relationships or eligibility waivers that could result in athletes being nearly the same age as young assistant coaches.

It’s an example of the gamesmanship in which some witnesses will engage when grilled by lawyers during depositions. The lawyer wanted to get an admission from Alberts that could be used broadly, and repeatedly, in the case arising from a relationship between a women’s basketball player and an assistant coach. Alberts tried to resist by clumsily splitting hairs.

Then there’s the question of whether Michigan is genuinely troubled by the testimony, or whether the school is using it as a way to justify not hiring Alberts — and not paying him.

Whatever happens between Alberts and Michigan, it’s another example of the so-called “chaos” in college sports being not the result of players doing their best in trying to get paid but schools doing their worst in allocating resources.

If Michigan’s concerns about the testimony are legitimate and not a pretext for hiring someone else, it shows that Michigan engaged in poor vetting of Alberts before signing him to a memo of understanding. If it’s a pretext, it shows that Michigan made a hasty decision and then tried to justify a change by trumping up testimony that otherwise wouldn’t have been an issue for Michigan.

Regardless, the possibility that Michigan will owe Alberts all or part of $17.2 million is another tangible example of the fact that the “chaos” comes not from players making good decisions but from schools making bad ones.

#2613. Celtics HC Joe Mazzulla reaches Phil Jackson level with win vs. Wizards

Celtics HC Joe Mazzulla reaches Phil Jackson level with win vs. Wizards appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Just days ago, the Boston Celtics suffered a franchise-low loss against the Sacramento Kings. Another loss to the Golden State Warriors further extended their struggles. However, the Celtics bounced back in stellar fashion on Tuesday. The Celtics secured a 131-116 victory over the Washington Wizards, with head coach Joe Mazzull a also reaching a significant career milestone.

With Tuesday night's victory over the Wizards, Mazzulla recorded his 250th career coaching win. According to Celtics Stats, Mazzulla tied Phil Jackson as the second-fastest coach in NBA history to reach 250 victories. Only Steve Kerr reached the milestone faster, doing so in 302 games.

Moreover, Mazzulla is now one of only four coaches to record 250 wins within their first 350 games. He achieved the milestone in 346 games, matching Jackson, while Pat Riley reached the mark in 348 games.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

The Celtics entered the matchup hoping to regain their rhythm after a series of disappointing performances. Their victory over Washington ultimately helped them snap a four-game losing streak and get their season back on track.

Jayson Tatum was undoubtedly the biggest difference-maker for Boston. The Celtics superstar delivered an outstanding performance, finishing with 41 points, six rebounds and eight assists while shooting 13-of-25 from the field and 6-of-15 from beyond the arc.

Paul George also played a major role in the victory, contributing 28 points, four rebounds and nine assists. Meanwhile, Neemias Queta provided a spark, recording 21 points on an efficient 9-of-11 shooting from the field.

The victory carries additional significance for Boston beyond simply ending its losing streak. With the win, the Celtics improved to 3-0 in NBA Cup group play while moving to 12-6 overall.

Boston will now shift its focus to Friday's matchup against the Atlanta Hawks. Another victory would secure the top spot in Eastern Conference Group C and further strengthen the Celtics' position heading into the NBA Cup knockout stage.
